## 2026-08-04 — Bind Go receiver fields to constructor-selected instances

**Decision.** Extend Go object-authorization method summaries with receiver-instance requirements. A keyed constructor composite may initialize a named pointer or basic-interface field from one exact constructor parameter or direct concrete expression. The caller resolves the actual argument, carries its exact module/package/type and pointer identity through bounded aliases and assignments, and stores that proof under the returned receiver's field map. A summary through that field is usable only when its instance requirements are satisfied at the final call site.

**Why.** Pointer and interface declarations identify static access paths but do not prove non-nil state or a runtime implementation. Constructors are the common place where Go programs establish that state. Enumerating every visible interface implementation without carrying the selected argument would create false positives; choosing a single visible implementation would create false negatives and unstable results. Parameterized receiver requirements let the graph retain all exact alternatives and defer narrowing until the concrete constructor call provides the missing instance fact.

**Constructor and method-set boundary.** Parse only keyed fields in the constructor's single exact local composite result. A source may be one non-reassigned parameter or a directly resolvable composite, allocation, interface conversion, or nested exact constructor within the existing eight-step constructor bound. Concrete fields require exact static parameter and field identity. Interface parameters must resolve to the same local basic interface when both sides are interfaces. The call must satisfy exact ordinary or minimum variadic arity, and a bare argument may use only a live exact binding. An interface-held value cannot select a pointer-only method; a pointer may select pointer or value methods. Missing fields, nil or unbound values, wrong types, reassigned parameters, ambiguous constructors or structs, and unsatisfied instance requirements fail closed.

**Summary and ambiguity boundary.** A field edge records both the static declaration and a field-path requirement. Interface dispatch may produce several exact receiver-specific summaries; ambiguous paths without instance requirements still fail closed. Candidate grouping retains multiple alternatives only when every alternative is guarded by a concrete receiver requirement. Final matching traverses the constructor-produced field map, compares canonical directory, package, type, and pointer mode, and emits only satisfied summaries. Constructor-field proof is added to the evidence path, and unused implementations never appear in the finding.

## 2026-08-04 — Resolve exact Go constructors and concrete receiver fields

**Decision.** Extend the bounded Go object-authorization method graph through receivers obtained from one exact constructor and through named concrete value fields. A constructor must be one uniquely defined non-method function in the same package or an authoritative local module, declare one exact local struct result, and return a matching composite directly or through at most eight top-level aliases. A method selector may traverse at most eight named non-pointer concrete value fields, resolving each field type in the field declaration's own file and import context. **Why.** Ordinary Go handlers frequently obtain a service from `NewService`, while service methods delegate through a repository stored in a struct field. The prior method graph rejected both patterns even when the returned service type and repository field type were statically exact. Go selectors operate over the declared field/method chain, but a pointer or interface field's declared type does not prove a live concrete instance. Direct constructor result identity and concrete value fields therefore provide a useful exact boundary without inventing heap state, dependency-injection behavior, or a dynamic implementation.

**Constructor boundary.** Parse only a single exact result type that names a unique local non-generic struct. Require the call to satisfy the exact ordinary argument count or the minimum variadic arity, and require exactly one function-level return. The returned expression must be a matching `T{...}` or `&T{...}` composite, either directly or through at most eight top-level single-name aliases. **Field and selector boundary.** Parse only unique non-generic struct declarations whose relevant fields are individually named with one simple concrete type. Resolve an unqualified field type in its declaring package and a qualified type through that declaration file's exact import, not the method file's possibly different aliases. Mask non-semantic struct tags without discarding the named field. Follow at most eight fields and retain each declaration as `go-method-receiver-field` evidence before the downstream method argument. Accept a pointer receiver method on the final value field because that field is addressable through the proven receiver. Reject pointer and interface fields because the declaration alone does not prove non-nil or dynamic instance identity; also reject embedded, promoted, anonymous, duplicate, generic, missing, ambiguous, and ninth fields.

## 2026-08-04 — Resolve exact Go method and bound-interface object paths

**Decision.** Extend the bounded Go object-authorization summary graph from functions into uniquely defined concrete methods. Resolve a selector only from a proven same-package or exact local-module receiver type. **Why.** Go services and repositories are commonly methods even when their security-relevant object and principal values remain explicit parameters. Rejecting every receiver lost straightforward handler-to-service-to-repository paths. The Go specification makes `T`, `*T`, and interface method sets materially different. CodeQL's official Go library distinguishes the declared target of an interface call from all possible concrete runtime callees, while gosec uses AST, SSA, and taint analyzers but does not publish a dedicated object-authorization rule. The correct standalone boundary is therefore exact local receiver provenance, not selector spelling or an assumption that one visible implementation is the runtime target.

**Receiver and module boundary.** A method declaration must have one simple named receiver type, with or without an explicit receiver variable, and be unique by directory, package, receiver type, and method name. **Interface and dynamic-dispatch boundary.** Parse only local basic interfaces with directly declared methods. A local interface variable must receive one exact concrete value through a typed declaration, later exact assignment, or explicit interface conversion; preserve its static interface identity across exact assignments and aliases. Each method edge additionally records `go-method-receiver-parameter`, `go-method-receiver-binding`, `go-method-receiver-alias`, or `go-interface-receiver-binding` before the existing object argument, string parameter, alias, SQL, and sink evidence. Principal positions continue to map edge by edge; an attacker header still removes the principal control. ## 2026-08-04 — Compose exact Go object wrappers across packages

**Decision.** Replace the Go object-authorization model's single-wrapper boundary with a bounded summary graph over exact same-package and repository-local cross-package functions. Compose at most 32 wrapper boundaries, preserve each source-to-argument, parameter, alias, and delegation location, and map authenticated-principal positions through the complete chain before applying the existing fixed-query, returned-data, mutation, statement, transaction, and authorization controls.

**Why.** Ordinary Go services split handlers, application services, and repositories across packages. The previous one-wrapper model lost an otherwise explicit path as soon as a service forwarded the object key to a repository. CodeQL's official Go data-flow documentation distinguishes local flow from configurable global interprocedural flow and its library exposes call-graph targets; Semgrep documents per-file Community Edition and cross-file or cross-function proprietary analysis. The current CodeQL Go query index and published gosec rule families do not expose a dedicated object-level authorization query. The useful product boundary is therefore a deterministic standalone proof path with explicit failure and evidence semantics, not a claim that other analyzers lack interprocedural machinery.

**Callable and module boundary.** Build descriptors only for unique non-method functions with exact string parameters. A same-package edge binds by directory, package, callable, and argument position. A qualified edge also requires the deepest enclosing `go.mod`, exact local-module import path, ordinary unique import or renamed alias, exported unique target, and an unshadowed package binding. The call must be top-level, non-deferred, and non-goroutine. Reject missing or duplicate module identities, wrong or external imports, ambiguous definitions, methods, local or parameter ownership of the callable name, package shadowing, nested calls, and dynamically selected function values.

**Object, principal, and evidence boundary.** Track only exact parameter flow through top-level single-name assignments and recognized string-preserving expressions. Retain every outer call, string parameter declaration, local object alias, internal delegation, leaf propagator, query control, and protected sink at its true file and line. Remap principal parameter indexes edge by edge, and retain a principal-bound query only when a value derived from the request context reaches the exact leaf predicate; a header, query value, or principal-shaped name is not sufficient. Immutable map selection, multi-name tainted assignment, a nested identity touch, dependence on more than one parameter, or reassignment to a fixed object is a barrier.

**Ambiguity and resource boundary.** Compose only simple paths and reject cycles. If multiple routes reach the same leaf sink, suppress that result rather than choose one evidence path; distinct unique sinks remain reviewable. Accept exactly 32 wrapper boundaries and reject the thirty-third. Stop after 4,096 candidate paths and fall back to independently proven direct summaries. Final handler calls use the same unique callable identity check, preventing duplicate local-module targets from being accepted after summary construction.

**Executable and regression evidence.** Expand the strict manifest from twenty to twenty-two cases with paired Go 1.26 modules. A handler imports a service, the service aliases the object ID and imports a repository, and the repository executes fixed parameterized deletion. The positive omits principal scope and a deterministic driver proves a victim invoice is deleted. The control preserves that topology and adds only a context-derived account predicate, proving the victim survives and an owned invoice still deletes. Focused regressions cover same-package and imported chains, renamed aliases, exact object and principal propagation, attacker-provided principal rejection, missing and wrong module identity, package shadowing, duplicate modules and paths, cycles, nested calls, callable-parameter shadowing, immutable selection, fixed replacement, exact 32-versus-33 depth, and line-accurate fixture evidence. ## 2026-08-04 — Resolve exact transaction factory helper chains

**Decision.** Extend the Go object-authorization transaction state machine across exact local factory functions that return a newly created `database/sql` transaction. ## 2026-08-04 — Compose exact transaction finalizer helper chains

**Decision.** Resolve typed same-package transaction finalizer summaries as a bounded, cycle-safe function graph. A summary may forward an exact `database/sql` transaction parameter or a proven live local alias through uniquely resolved non-method functions until one eligible top-level `Commit` or `Rollback` is reached. Stopping after one helper misses an otherwise explicit durable effect. Treating every transaction-shaped call as equivalent would instead admit name lookalikes, wrong arguments, recursive graphs, branch-confined finalizers, and multiple outcomes. **Graph, identity, and resilience boundary.** Build descriptors only for unique non-method functions in the same package directory with an exact imported `database/sql` transaction parameter. At each non-deferred function-level call, require the callee summary's transaction argument to resolve to exactly one live caller parameter identity. Track simple local aliases and remove identities on reassignment. Recursively compose one leaf outcome, memoize resolved summaries, reject cycles and ambiguous callees, and stop beyond 32 boundaries to keep hostile or generated source from exhausting the host. Multiple direct or delegated finalizers make the function ambiguous. A call to an ambiguous helper invalidates the path only when it receives the caller's transaction; unrelated local transactions do not erase an independent exact outcome.

**Evidence and control-flow boundary.** Emit `go-sql-transaction-finalizer-helper` for the application call and every internal delegation, each with its real path, line, and callee symbol. Apply rollback chains with the same bounded dominance rule as direct rollback: a function-level call clears the pending mutation, while an error-branch call does not dominate a later success commit. Nested or deferred forwarding, mixed outcomes, parameter or alias reassignment, wrong transaction position, recursion, and over-depth chains fail closed. A commit call remains intended closure evidence rather than proof that commit succeeded.

## 2026-08-04 — Summarize exact transaction finalizer helpers

**Decision.** Allow a pending typed Go object mutation to close through one uniquely resolved same-package function when an exact `database/sql` transaction parameter reaches exactly one non-deferred function-level `Commit` or `Rollback`. A narrow semantic summary therefore improves recall without promoting arbitrary similarly named calls or claiming whole-program control-flow precision.

**Resolution and ordering boundary.** Summarize only non-method functions in the same package directory with one unambiguous definition. Preserve transaction aliases. Reject duplicate definitions, methods, untyped or lookalike parameters, another transaction, caller or helper nesting, caller or helper defer, mixed top-level commit and rollback, finalization before execution, and calls with finalizer arguments. Ignore an error-branch rollback helper when a later top-level success commit exists; apply a top-level rollback helper as dominating finalization.

The committed-mutation sink remains at the caller boundary because that is where the application requests finalization; its propagator shows where the standard-library operation occurs. This proves intended durable closure, not successful commit. Review returned errors, context cancellation, panic/recover behavior, deferred ordering, retries, driver behavior, constraints, triggers, row-level security, and observed state before final impact claims.

## 2026-08-04 — Preserve prepared SQL across exact transaction statement transfer

**Decision.** Model `database/sql` `Tx.Stmt` and `Tx.StmtContext` as an exact transfer from a known active prepared statement into a distinct returned statement bound to the receiving transaction. Copy the fixed mutation predicates and original preparation line, add a transfer propagator at the call, and queue later execution on that transaction until its exact commit closure. Do not mutate the identity or outcome behavior of the original DB statement.

**Why.** Go provides these methods specifically to derive a transaction-specific statement from an existing prepared statement. The returned statement operates within the transaction and closes when that transaction commits or rolls back; `StmtContext` uses its context for preparation, not execution. Losing the predicate at this boundary misses a documented application pattern. Treating the source statement itself as transaction-bound would instead create false behavior for later DB execution, while accepting a method name without exact source, returned result, and transaction identities would broaden into unrelated APIs and dead transfers.

**Identity and lifecycle boundary.** Require an active proven transaction receiver, exact `Stmt` argument zero or `StmtContext` argument one, and a known active prepared source. An assigned result gets a durable local identity; the documented direct `tx.Stmt(...).Exec(...)` form is accepted only when the execution call's exact same-expression prefix matches that transfer. A DB-prepared source may transfer; a source already bound to the same transaction may transfer; a source bound to another transaction may not. The returned statement gets independent close/reassignment state but references the destination transaction's shared outcome state. Statement and transaction aliases preserve their respective objects. Ignore an unused return, an unrelated execution after an ignored transfer even on the same line, unknown or closed source, reversed context/source arguments, cross-transaction source, closed or replaced result, execution after finalization, rollback, or an uncommitted pending mutation. The original statement remains executable as an immediate DB mutation and closing it after transfer does not retroactively erase the returned transaction statement.

**Evidence and reviewer boundary.** Preserve the request source, wrapper argument and parameter, original prepare location, exact transfer location and returned symbol, object and optional security predicates, execution arguments, destination transaction identity, and commit location. A context passed to `StmtContext` is neither the statement source nor authorization proof. A commit call is still evidence of intended durable closure rather than proof that commit succeeded; review returned errors, context cancellation, driver behavior, database constraints and triggers, transaction options, row-level security, and observed state before final impact claims.

**Executable and regression evidence.** Expand the strict manifest from eight to ten cases with paired Go 1.26 modules and deterministic drivers. Both prepare on the DB, transfer with `Tx.StmtContext`, execute through the returned statement, and stage state until commit. The positive proves a victim invoice is durably deleted. The control changes only the account predicate and context-principal value, proving the cross-account request commits no deletion while an owned request still commits successfully. Focused tests additionally cover both transfer methods, assigned and direct-chained execution, unrelated same-line calls, exact context/source position, independent original and clone behavior, ignored returns, closed, replaced, or unknown statements, distinct transactions, aliases, rollback, no commit, and exact propagator order. Hosted Linux CI runs both witnesses.

**Acceptance evidence.** The focused authorization lane passes 31 tests and 173 assertions; all eleven typed Go suites pass 194 tests and 1,043 assertions; all ten authorization witnesses pass. The authoritative Windows suite passes 987 tests with 19 intentional skips, no failures, and 7,690 assertions across 117 files. Compiled-output review emits exactly one positive at `store.go:25`, preserving source `handler.go:9`, wrapper argument `handler.go:10`, string parameter and prepare `store.go:8-9`, transfer `store.go:20`, object predicate `store.go:9`, execution `store.go:22`, and commit `store.go:25`; the control retains only the context-principal query control at execution, and production SDK source emits no Go authorization record. Formatting, generated models, TypeScript checking, the production build, dependency audit, strict package inspection, isolated install/import/CLI, and all 79 bundled-plugin checks are clean. The validated 251-entry archive is 1,324,131 bytes packed and 5,961,762 bytes unpacked, has SHA-1 `50261671b78d988819591218ba8c821244311bc1`, integrity `sha512-+67p89aDsZ+xRPUAzT2P9JwHPdDAhfMiIGKleL0QX3kAXJEWJFub0MMwkm4QFHggTAlUt0PjZjPDvGapfnrRxg==`, and was removed.

**Consequence.** The model now covers direct transaction execution, transaction-prepared statements, and assigned or directly chained DB-prepared statements transferred into a transaction without relaxing receiver or object identity. Remaining work includes helper-mediated transfer, branch-sensitive commit success, returned or struct-held statements and transactions, savepoints, retries, driver-specific statement wrappers, ORM unit-of-work abstractions, and database-enforced authorization. Each extension must preserve the prepared query, exact statement and transaction identities, execution order, principal provenance, commit outcome, and protected-state effect.

## 2026-08-04 — Require transaction outcome before claiming a Go mutation effect

**Decision.** Make the typed Go object-authorization model transaction aware. A request-selected `UPDATE` or `DELETE` dispatched on an exact `*sql.Tx`, including a statement prepared by that transaction, remains provisional until the same transaction reaches a non-deferred function-level `Commit`. Emit a distinct `go-database-object-committed-mutation` sink at commit and retain execution as a propagator. Discard pending mutation evidence on a dominating function-level `Rollback`; do not report an unfinalized transaction as durable state change.

**Why.** Go defines `Tx` as an in-progress transaction that must end in `Commit` or `Rollback`. `Rollback` aborts the transaction; after either finalizer, later operations fail with `ErrTxDone`; statements prepared on a transaction close with it. `BeginTx` also binds its context through finalization and automatically rolls back if that context is canceled, causing commit to return an error. Reporting at `Tx.Exec` therefore overstates paths that roll back or never commit. Conversely, rejecting every function containing rollback would miss the documented and idiomatic `defer tx.Rollback()` plus later commit pattern and ordinary error-branch rollback followed by success-path commit.

**Identity and ordering boundary.** Initialize transaction state for exact typed `*sql.Tx` parameters and first results of `Begin` or `BeginTx` on proven DB, Tx, or Conn receivers. Preserve simple local aliases as references to the same active state and clear a name on reassignment. Queue direct or transaction-prepared mutation sinks instead of emitting them. Accept `Commit` only at function nesting depth one, including `return tx.Commit()` and an `if err := tx.Commit(); ...` initializer where the call itself executes unconditionally. Reject commit before mutation, commit inside a nested conditional, deferred commit, and operations after finalization. A non-deferred depth-one rollback clears pending state; deferred rollback and rollback inside an error branch do not dominate the later success path. This is bounded structural dominance, not a claim of full control-flow analysis.

**Object, control, and reviewer boundary.** Preserve the exact request source, wrapper, fixed object predicate, placeholder-to-argument mapping, mutation dispatch, transaction identity, and commit location. Security predicates remain controls only when their exact execution values derive from the authenticated request context. The commit call is strong durable-effect evidence but not proof that commit succeeded: correction must inspect its returned error, `BeginTx` context cancellation or deadline, driver behavior, transaction options, database constraints/triggers, authorization predicates, and observed state. Direct DB and Conn mutations remain immediate database operations; reads on a transaction remain disclosable before finalization and are not suppressed by rollback.

**Executable and regression evidence.** Expand the strict Go authorization manifest from six to eight cases with paired Go 1.26 modules and deterministic transactional drivers. The positive stages deletion of a path-selected victim invoice inside `BeginTx`/`Tx.ExecContext`, applies it only in driver `Commit`, and proves the victim record is gone. The control preserves the route, wrapper, transaction, attack ID, execution, commit, and response while adding an account predicate bound from request context; it proves the victim survives the committed attack transaction and the caller can commit deletion of its own invoice. Focused tests cover direct and prepared Tx mutations, typed and derived transaction identity, aliases, direct/return/if-initializer commit, deferred rollback, error-branch rollback, unconditional rollback, missing or premature finalization, nested and deferred commit, operations after finalization, principal controls, strict manifest expectations, and exact evidence paths. Hosted Linux CI executes both witnesses.

**Acceptance evidence.** The expanded focused authorization lane passes 26 tests and 130 assertions, and all eleven typed Go suites pass 189 tests and 1,000 assertions. All eight authorization witnesses pass. The authoritative Windows suite passes 982 tests with 19 intentional skips, no failures, and 7,647 assertions across 117 files. Compiled-output review emits exactly one positive at `store.go:22`, preserving source `handler.go:9`, wrapper argument `handler.go:10`, string parameter `store.go:8`, object predicate and transaction execution `store.go:19`, and commit `store.go:22`; the control carries only the context-principal query control at execution, and production SDK source emits no committed transaction record. Formatting, generated models, TypeScript checking, the production build, dependency audit, strict package inspection, isolated install/import/CLI, and all 79 bundled-plugin checks are clean. The validated 251-entry archive is 1,333,119 bytes packed and 5,952,556 bytes unpacked, has SHA-1 `c7d1faa7043207ddd9cece594a09f3bcef14425e`, integrity `sha512-uqRvdONMNrG3PZLXZLAlkD7nQGfGDcq/cxMINS15zeLIoc/Bm7+QFuV6scurXnwP1BUF3E1cWviWUBubZPRByQ==`, and was removed.

**Consequence.** Transactional object mutation findings now explain durable closure and eliminate known rollback/no-commit false positives without discarding idiomatic success paths. Remaining work includes commit/rollback through helper functions or multiple wrappers, branch-sensitive finalization beyond bounded nesting, returned or struct/package-held transactions, savepoints, context/deadline value analysis, commit-error dominance, panic/recover and deferred-finalizer ordering, transaction retries, distributed or nested transactions, ORM unit-of-work abstractions, database triggers, and row-level security. Each extension must preserve transaction identity, execution order, commit success, principal provenance, exact object/action scope, and observed protected-state outcome.

## 2026-08-04 — Close Go object mutations through prepared-statement identity

**Decision.** Extend the typed Go object-authorization model from direct DB, Tx, and Conn `Exec` mutations into fixed `UPDATE` and `DELETE` statements created with `Prepare` or `PrepareContext` and later dispatched through the exact returned `Stmt.Exec` or `Stmt.ExecContext`. Preserve preparation, parsed object/security predicates, statement identity, execution arguments, principal binding, and mutation dispatch as separate evidence. Preparation alone is inert.

**Why.** Go documents that `Prepare` creates a statement for later query or execution, that `PrepareContext` uses its context for preparation rather than execution, and that `Stmt.Exec` and `Stmt.ExecContext` later execute the prepared statement with their own argument roles. Treating preparation as an effect would over-report dead code; considering only direct DB execution leaves a common prepared mutation path invisible. The current CodeQL Go query inventory and published gosec rule surface expose injection analysis but no dedicated Go object-level authorization query, so exact host-side lifecycle closure adds recall without confusing bound values with SQL grammar.

**Lifecycle boundary.** Accept only an exact default or aliased `database/sql` import, a proven DB, Tx, or Conn receiver, a fixed query, and the first returned statement value. `Prepare` takes SQL at argument zero; `PrepareContext` takes it at argument one. Store only statements whose first SQL action token is exactly `UPDATE` or `DELETE`, preserving the parsed placeholder map. Follow a simple local alias only while neither name is reassigned. A non-deferred `Close` through any proven alias invalidates the shared statement before later dispatch; deferred cleanup remains after the execution point. Reject preparation without execution, different statements, replacement, SELECT/INSERT and action-prefix lookalikes, untyped receivers, and ambiguous query text.

**Object, control, and effect boundary.** At `Stmt.Exec`, map placeholders from argument zero; at `Stmt.ExecContext`, skip the execution context and map from argument one. Require an exact object-key equality predicate whose execution value traces to typed request input. A security predicate counts as `principal-bound-object-query` only when its exact execution argument traces to a context-derived authenticated principal; headers and other request values remain attacker controlled. Emit the existing `go-database-object-mutation` sink at execution, with separate prepare, object-predicate, and statement-execution propagators. Direct Exec retains the same object/control boundary and now uses an exact action-token test plus explicit mutation-dispatch evidence.

**Executable and regression evidence.** Expand the strict Go authorization manifest from four to six cases with paired Go 1.26 modules. The positive sends a path-selected victim invoice through one wrapper into `PrepareContext` and `Stmt.ExecContext`; its offline `database/sql/driver` statement proves that the victim record is deleted. The control preserves route, wrapper, statement lifecycle, attack ID, and response, but adds an account predicate bound from request context; its driver proves the victim survives while the caller's own invoice can still be deleted. Focused tests cover both prepare and execution variants, DB/Tx/Conn identity, exact aliases, deferred cleanup, direct and alias close, replacement, unrelated or absent dispatch, exact mutation tokens, named and positional values, attacker-controlled security fields, principal controls, wrapper evidence, and strict manifest expectations. Hosted Linux CI executes both witnesses.

All six authorization witnesses pass. The authoritative Windows suite passes 977 tests with 19 intentional skips, no failures, and 7,616 assertions across 117 files. Compiled-output review emits exactly one positive at `store.go:19`, preserving source `handler.go:9`, wrapper argument `handler.go:10`, string parameter `store.go:8`, prepare and predicate `store.go:13`, and statement execution `store.go:19`; the control carries only the context-principal query control at its execution line, and production SDK source emits none. Formatting, generated models, TypeScript checking, the production build, dependency audit, strict package inspection, isolated install/import/CLI, and all 79 bundled-plugin checks are clean. The validated 251-entry archive is 1,330,790 bytes packed and 5,941,585 bytes unpacked, has SHA-1 `ee8b2d70e688ec7ffb3c881171c4bd7fd78ce93c`, integrity `sha512-cIzecH5UPjCiHi7/7wCKRU++5JIbtnK0fO39dkq4OUNWaARjIMotbnKIItLeCmbbpXZagkJRjsBo/zG2mWcGMA==`, and was removed.

**Consequence.** Review now receives a complete request-object-to-prepared-mutation path rather than either missing the action or reporting inert preparation. Remaining extensions include package-level or struct-held statements, `Tx.Stmt`/`StmtContext`, statement creation in constructors, cross-package and multiple wrapper hops, branch-sensitive close and reassignment, transaction commit/rollback dominance, optimistic-concurrency predicates, policy-service decisions, database row-level security, bulk mutations, joins/composite keys, sqlc/sqlx/builders/ORM mutation APIs, and exact action/role scope. ## 2026-08-04 — Close Go collection authorization through exact Rows iteration

**Decision.** Extend the typed Go object-authorization model from single-row `QueryRow` disclosure into collection access through exact standard-library `Query`/`QueryContext`, returned `Rows` identity, conditional `Next`, `Scan`, and response use of scanned data. Emit a distinct collection-response sink so list/export impact, single-record impact, and mutations remain independently reviewable. Accept security-named request keys as possible selected objects while continuing to require authenticated-principal provenance for any security predicate claimed as authorization evidence.

**Why.** Go documents that DB, Tx, and Conn `Query` methods return `*Rows`, that the cursor begins before its first record, that `Next` prepares a row for reading, and that every `Rows.Scan` must be preceded by `Next`. Query execution, cursor creation, or a same-named scan therefore does not prove collection disclosure. OWASP API1:2023 explicitly uses a shop revenue listing selected by a request path as a BOLA example, so restricting the model to at-most-one-row APIs left a material false-negative family. Conversely, broadly treating any later `Scan` or writer as closure would discard the exact object-lifecycle discipline that makes the single-record model useful.

**Cursor and disclosure boundary.** Extend fixed-query positions to `Query` at argument zero and `QueryContext` at argument one on proven DB, Tx, or Conn receivers. Store only the exact first returned value as a `Rows` candidate, preserve a simple non-reassigned local cursor alias, and clear identity when an assigned name is replaced. Require `Next` on that cursor in an `if` or `for` condition before accepting receiver `Scan`; a bare ignored `Next`, a different receiver, a later unrelated bare `Scan`, `Close`, `Err`, or query construction alone is inert. Preserve scanned values and bounded aliases only until one reaches an exact writer/encoder shape. Direct chained `QueryRow(...).Scan(...)` remains supported only when parse and scan share the same line; assigned `Row.Scan` requires the exact returned receiver.

**Object and control boundary.** Any exact `id`, `key`, `uuid`, or suffixed key predicate may select the attacker-chosen object, including names such as `account_id`. Naming does not make request input trusted. A security-dimension predicate becomes `principal-bound-object-query` only when its exact placeholder value traces to `Request.Context().Value` through a principal-related server-owned key; a request header, query, form, path value, or similarly named wrapper argument remains attacker controlled. When several request-derived keys reach one wrapper sink, emit one protected-effect hypothesis rather than duplicate reports by parameter. Preserve positional, numbered, and named placeholder-to-argument mapping and require the reviewer to validate the exact collection, action, denial behavior, and returned properties.

**Executable and regression evidence.** Expand the strict authorization manifest from two to four cases with paired Go 1.26 collection modules. The positive sends `victim-project` through a typed path source and one unique wrapper into `SELECT secret FROM invoices WHERE project_id = ?`, then traverses `QueryContext`, `Rows.Next`, `Rows.Scan`, and `io.WriteString`; a deterministic offline driver proves disclosure of both victim secrets. The control preserves the route, project ID, wrapper, cursor loop, scan, and response but adds `account_id = ?` bound from the authenticated request context; its driver proves the attacker sees no victim data and still receives its owned project record. Sixteen focused tests cover the four-case manifest, exact single and collection paths, Query/QueryContext roles, cursor aliases, Next/Scan/disclosure closure, ignored Next, missing or unrelated Scan, generic responses, context-only controls, security-named object keys, DB/Tx/Conn identity, wrappers, deduplication, reassignment, fixed maps, comments/strings, and reviewer guidance. Hosted Linux CI executes all four authorization modules.

The authoritative Windows suite passes 972 tests with 19 intentional skips, no failures, and 7,589 assertions across 117 files. Built-output review retains the original pair and emits one collection hypothesis at `store.go:22`, preserving source `handler.go:9`, wrapper argument `handler.go:10`, parameter `store.go:10`, object predicate `store.go:11`, iteration `store.go:17`, scan `store.go:19`, and response `store.go:22`; the safe collection carries only the context-principal query control at `store.go:11`, and production SDK source emits none. Formatting, generated models, TypeScript checking, the clean build, Go formatting/execution, strict package inspection, isolated install/import/CLI, all 79 bundled-plugin checks, and the production advisory query are clean. The 251-entry archive is 1,317,171 bytes, has SHA-1 `e1e77e48d06826ab259a478a8427dae7d2047010`, integrity `sha512-3GEcmAFkifn4vdXzweDSUjmE+ev+w6uVOAUzFJIkG1B/xk+68NL9Vouekv9CZshOzWXUD4LENBSu+7rHLvw+3w==`, passed validation, and was removed.

**Consequence.** The Go authorization lane now covers both at-most-one-row and collection disclosure with explicit result-object lifecycle, reducing false negatives without adopting loose API-name matching. Remaining work includes nested and multi-result-set loops, `Next` state across branch joins, slice/struct collection accumulation and later serialization, JSON encoders whose data is assembled outside the loop, helper methods around `Rows`, package-level queries, receiver methods and fields, cross-package or multiple wrapper hops, joins and composite keys, row mappers, policy services, sqlc/sqlx/builders/ORM repositories, transaction-coupled mutations, middleware context definitions, database row-level security, and deployment action scope. ## 2026-08-04 — Require exact Go object, principal, query, and effect closure

**Decision.** Add a separate typed Go CWE-639/CWE-862 model for object-level authorization at the standard-library `net/http` to `database/sql` boundary. Preserve request source, object identifier, fixed query and placeholder role, exact typed receiver, wrapper argument, authenticated-principal provenance, candidate authorization control, selected data, and protected read or mutation effect as distinct evidence. Parameterized SQL prevents grammar injection; it does not authorize the object selected by a bound value.

**Why.** OWASP API1:2023 requires every endpoint receiving an object ID and acting on the selected record to validate that the logged-in principal may perform the exact action on that object, whether the identifier is an integer, UUID, or string and whether the effect is read, update, or delete. Go documents `QueryRow`/`QueryRowContext`, later `Scan`, and the separation between fixed query text and placeholder arguments, including multi-predicate examples. The current CodeQL Go query index and published gosec rule list do not expose a dedicated object-level authorization query. A precise standard-library lane therefore adds material coverage without conflating BOLA with SQL injection, endpoint authentication, UUID opacity, or generic business-logic suspicion.

**Type, source, and query boundary.** Require exact default or explicit `net/http` and `database/sql` imports and a typed `*http.Request` plus proven DB, Tx, or Conn receiver. Accept query, form, path, and header object identifiers and preserve bounded assignments, fixed query constants, direct calls, inferred handles, and one unique same-package string wrapper. Parse equality predicates with `?`, `$n`, `:name`, or `@name` placeholders, including exact `sql.Named` values. Treat `id`, `key`, `uuid`, and their suffixed column forms as object keys while keeping owner, tenant, account, organization, customer, principal, shop, user, and workspace keys as security dimensions. Dynamic queries, fixed/reassigned identifiers, immutable server-owned maps, untyped lookalikes, duplicate wrappers, comments, and strings fail closed. Shared call parsing now tracks quote and escape state so commas inside interpreted or raw Go string arguments do not split callable roles.

**Principal, control, and effect boundary.** A principal is trusted only when it comes from `Request.Context().Value` under a principal-related server-owned key and remains non-reassigned. A same-query security predicate is recorded as `principal-bound-object-query` only when its exact placeholder argument has that provenance, including across a unique wrapper; a similarly named string, header, form, path, or query argument is not control evidence. A returned owner-like value can produce `post-lookup-principal-check` only when it is scanned, compared with the context principal, and a fail-closed return precedes disclosure. A read requires exact `QueryRow` construction, `Scan`, and response use of scanned data through writer, `io`, `fmt`, or JSON encoder shapes. `UPDATE` and `DELETE` through exact `Exec` variants are immediate mutation effects. Generic success text, lookup without scan, checks after disclosure, and authorization for another object are excluded.

**Executable and regression evidence.** Add paired Go 1.26 modules with deterministic offline `database/sql/driver` witnesses. The positive takes a path-selected victim invoice through one wrapper into `SELECT secret FROM invoices WHERE id = ?`, scans it, and proves disclosure of `victim-signing-key`. The control preserves the handler, wrapper, object ID, query dispatch, scan, and response but binds `account_id` to a context-derived authenticated account. Its witness proves an attacker account receives no victim record while its owned invoice still returns normally. Fourteen focused tests cover the perfect manifest, exact cross-file path and evidence locations, every typed request family, read closure, generic-response rejection, update/delete effects, context-only controls, positional/numbered/named placeholders, exact imports and typed receivers, fixed queries, DB/Tx/Conn and inferred handles, fail-closed post-lookup checks, immutable maps, ambiguity, wrapper principal provenance, reassignment, comments/strings, and reviewer guidance. Hosted Linux CI executes both modules.

**Acceptance evidence.** The focused lane passes 14 tests and 54 assertions, and all eleven typed Go suites pass 177 tests and 924 assertions. Both executable witnesses pass. The authoritative Windows suite passes 970 tests with 19 intentional skips, no failures, and 7,571 assertions across 117 files. Built-output review emits one positive at `store.go:16`, preserving the path source at `handler.go:9`, wrapper argument at `handler.go:10`, parameter at `store.go:9`, object predicate and row scan at `store.go:11`, and response at `store.go:16`; the safe fixture emits the same bounded hypothesis with only a context-principal query control at `store.go:11`, and production SDK source emits none. Formatting, generated models, TypeScript checking, the clean build, Go formatting/execution, strict package inspection, isolated install/import/CLI, all 79 bundled-plugin checks, and the production advisory query are clean. The 251-entry archive is 1,316,085 bytes, has SHA-1 `00714970e66caf7b3667f3fa203120e065d68bf5`, integrity `sha512-ey20scMPGPuG21Q6MabWnupTRGMQrmWjP3hXR6Se3YAiovgdZzwGDM0t6HdcB8C6FJuPa6bu1CrSMYUpLkL5kQ==`, passed validation, and was removed.

**Consequence.** The scanner now covers a high-impact Go authorization family absent from the inspected CodeQL and gosec surfaces while requiring more than endpoint, API-name, or principal-name heuristics. ## 2026-08-04 — Require Go template source to reach execution and retain capabilities

**Decision.** Add a separate typed Go CWE-1336 model for request-controlled source interpreted by the exact standard-library `text/template` package. Preserve typed request source, import identity, source argument zero, immutable source selection, builder and parsed-object identity, `FuncMap` registration, execution data, actual `Execute` or `ExecuteTemplate` closure, wrapper propagation, and concrete capability as distinct evidence. Parsing alone is not a reportable runtime effect, and rendering request data through a fixed server-owned template is not template-source injection.

**Why.** Go documents `Template.Parse` as parsing its string argument into template bodies and `Template.Funcs` as installing functions used by parsing and execution. The current CodeQL Go query index has no server-side template-injection query. The inspected gosec v2.25-era G708 taint configuration usefully covers `text/template`, but it reports tainted `Parse` without proving that the result executes, separately reports request data rendered through a fixed template as XSS under the same rule, and classifies `html.EscapeString`, `HTMLEscapeString`, query escaping, and path escaping as sanitizers. HTML escaping does not consume Go's brace-delimited directive grammar, and fixed-template output encoding is a separate browser-context question. Requiring parse-to-execute object closure improves precision while keeping escaping bypasses visible; retaining functions and execution data improves impact proof beyond API-name severity.

**Type, source, and lifecycle boundary.** Require one exact default or explicit `text/template` alias and reject dot, blank, duplicate, fork, suffix-lookalike, `html/template`, and locally shadowed bindings. Accept only query, indexed query, form, path, or header accessors on a typed `*net/http.Request`. Preserve direct source or bounded string assignments, separate `New` builders, `Funcs`, direct `Must(New(...).Parse(...)).Execute(...)` chains, builder and parsed-object aliases, self-returning builder calls, and source passed through one unique string function in the same package and directory. Clear source, builder, and parsed state on replacement. An exact immutable request-key-to-complete-source map is a deterministic barrier. Comments and strings are structurally masked, and ambiguous wrapper identities fail closed.

**Grammar, execution, and capability boundary.** Only argument zero to `Template.Parse` is template grammar. `Execute` data is not recursively parsed, and `ExecuteTemplate` keeps its writer, template-name, and data positions distinct. Store a tainted parse under the exact returned or mutated template object, require that same non-reassigned object to reach `Execute` or `ExecuteTemplate`, and record the execution line as the sink. Retain registered `FuncMap` and non-nil execution data as propagators rather than assuming a universal impact. Correction must reopen every registered function, exported data method, built-in, associated-template or recursion path, output destination and downstream consumer, secret, file/network/process/state capability, resource effect, error path, and deployment authority before assigning severity. Output autoescaping may control XSS; it cannot authorize attacker-supplied template grammar.

**Executable and regression evidence.** Add paired Go 1.26 modules. The positive takes a query value through one same-package wrapper, registers `readSigningKey`, parses the value, and executes the returned template; a real `httptest` request proves `{{readSigningKey}}` returns the internal fixture key. The control preserves the handler, wrapper, parameter name and payload, renderer, response, and key but parses a fixed `html/template` and supplies the request only as data; its test proves the directive stays literal, script markup is escaped, and the key is absent. Thirteen focused regressions cover the strict manifest, exact cross-file path, all typed request families, `ExecuteTemplate` argument roles, inert parsing, fixed source/data separation, non-sanitizing HTML/query/path escaping, immutable maps, exact aliases, `html/template` and package lookalikes, duplicate imports, local shadows, comments, strings, source and parsed-object replacement, builders, aliases, direct chains, `FuncMap` and data capabilities, unique wrappers, and reviewer guidance. Hosted Linux CI executes both modules.

The authoritative Windows suite passes 956 tests with 19 intentional skips, no failures, and 7,517 assertions across 116 files. ## 2026-08-04 — Preserve Go path taint through normalization and prove rooted controls

**Decision.** Add a separate typed Go request-to-filesystem model for exact standard-library `net/http`, `os`, legacy `io/ioutil`, `path/filepath`, and HTTP file-serving APIs. Preserve source, import identity, path construction, exact argument and source/destination role, filesystem effect, wrapper propagation, candidate controls, authorization, runtime/platform behavior, and concrete impact as distinct evidence. Keep lexical normalization and resolution tainted; accept immutable file selection and root-scoped filesystem APIs as counterevidence without silently declaring them universally safe.

**Why.** Go documents `filepath.Join` as cleaning its result, which means a later `..` component can escape the apparent base. `filepath.IsLocal` gives a useful lexical guarantee but explicitly does not account for symbolic links. `Clean`, `Abs`, `Rel`, and `EvalSymlinks` each transform or resolve a path without alone proving that the final operation stays within an authorized root under the actual filesystem and concurrent state. CodeQL's high-precision path-injection query recommends normalized relative containment or component validation. Gosec G304 covers a useful subset of open/read/create functions; G703 broadens its sinks but classifies `Clean`, `Abs`, `Base`, `Rel`, and `EvalSymlinks` as sanitizers. Retaining those operations as evidence lets the mandatory reviewer prove the real boundary rather than losing a path at a syntactic helper. GO-2026-4970/CVE-2026-39822, an affected-Unix trailing-slash symlink escape in `os.Root`/`OpenInRoot` fixed in Go 1.25.12 and 1.26.5, also demonstrates why API name alone cannot replace runtime and platform proof.

**Type, source, and sink boundary.** Require one exact default or explicit alias and reject dot, blank, duplicate, fork, and suffix-lookalike imports. Accept only typed `*http.Request` query, indexed query, form, path, or header accessors. Distinguish `os` and `io/ioutil` read, open, write/create, delete, metadata, link source/destination, move source/destination, and rooted-filesystem root-selection positions; `filepath.Walk`/`WalkDir` roots; and `http.ServeFile`/`ServeFileFS` response paths. Track direct expressions and assignments through relevant `filepath` construction. Clear replaced values and block direct or assigned request-key lookup through an immutable local string map. Follow at most one uniquely named string function in the same package and directory; ambiguity fails closed.

**Control and impact boundary.** Record lexical locality, valid slash-path conversion, basename reduction, normalization, relative containment, link resolution, absolute rejection, regular-expression allowlists, string checks, and root-scoped access as typed reviewer leads. None is promoted to authorization without proving it dominates the exact sink and constrains the same value. Request control of argument zero to `OpenRoot` or `OpenInRoot` is a root-selection finding; request-derived names under a fixed trusted root are excluded from ordinary unrooted sinks. The quality gate still requires the exact root, current patch level, platform behavior, symlink/junction/mount and attacker-writable-directory state, decoding and alternate separators, race resistance, file permissions, tenant/object policy, served content behavior, and the exact unauthorized read, overwrite/create, delete, move/link, metadata, traversal, or response exposure effect.

The control keeps the request, wrapper, file tree, payload, error handling, and allowed document but opens with `os.OpenInRoot`; its test proves traversal fails and `report.txt` still succeeds. ## 2026-08-04 — Extend Go process closure through manual state and direct dispatch

**Decision.** Extend the existing exact Go CWE-78 model through manually constructed `exec.Cmd` values and the standard-library `os.StartProcess`, `syscall.Exec`, `syscall.ForkExec`, and `syscall.StartProcess` entry points. Preserve package identity, source, executable selection, argv roles, mutable object or slice state, alias semantics, execution timing, command-specific interpretation, controls, and concrete effect as separate evidence. Manual command state requires later execution on the same object; low-level functions dispatch at the call itself.

**Why.** Go documents `Cmd.Path` as the executable path and `Cmd.Args` as the process argument vector, including `Args[0]`; an empty `Args` falls back to `Path`. `Run`, `Start`, `Output`, and `CombinedOutput` start a manually populated command just as they start one returned by `Command`. `os.StartProcess` is a low-level immediate interface, while `syscall.Exec`, `ForkExec`, and `StartProcess` directly replace or create a process. CodeQL's shipped `os/exec` sink model names only `Command` and `CommandContext`. Current gosec G204 and G702 include the low-level functions, but G204 flags unresolved arguments at construction and G702 treats each API as a general taint sink. Exact host modeling can close those missing surfaces while retaining the material distinction between executable choice, interpreted grammar, argv zero, and ordinary data arguments.

**Manual object and mutation boundary.** Recognize only exact default or aliased `os/exec` and execabs `Cmd` types. Parse balanced single-line or multiline composites, zero-value declarations, and `new(exec.Cmd)`. Track `Path`, complete `[]string` `Args` assignment, exact numeric `Args[index]` replacement, local slice literals and aliases, and exact slice-element replacement. A pointer alias shares later mutations; a copied value retains independent scalar fields, matching Go assignment semantics closely enough to avoid treating a later `Path` write on the original as a write to the copy. Reassignment clears manual state. Manual construction, field population, pipe setup, and `Wait` alone remain inert; only the four real execution methods close a finding. `Args[0]` is discarded before command-grammar role analysis because it does not replace `Path` as the executable.

**Direct-dispatch boundary.** Recognize one exact `os` import for `StartProcess` and one exact `syscall` import for `Exec`, `ForkExec`, or `StartProcess`; reject dot, blank, duplicate, fork, and suffix-lookalike imports. Argument zero is the executable and argument one is argv. Resolve exact inline or local `[]string` literals, aliases, and indexed element writes. The call itself closes dispatch—requiring a later method would create a false negative. A tainted executable reports selection; a fixed executable reuses the existing shell, interpreter, batch, SSH, Git, rsync, option terminator, and immutable-map grammar boundaries. Process-visible argv zero and fixed-program ordinary later arguments remain negative controls.

**Executable and regression evidence.** Add a second exploit/control pair beside the existing constructor pair. Both new modules retain the indexed HTTP source, same-package wrapper, `sh -c`, assigned command string, manual `Cmd`, complete `Args` assignment, `CombinedOutput`, witness, and attack bytes. Each copies its test executable to `sh` or `sh.exe`, points the manual command's working directory at that temporary location, and runs the child without invoking the host shell. The positive exposes the internal signing key; the immutable complete-command control rejects the injected key and still returns the intended public value. Focused regressions cover inert and executed composites, all field construction forms, exact field and slice mutation, pointer and value aliases, replacement, argv-zero exclusion, ordinary argv, all four low-level dispatchers, exact aliases and import ambiguity, a unique cross-file dispatcher wrapper, the paired manifest, and reviewer guidance.

Formatting, generated models, TypeScript checking, the clean build, Go formatting and execution, and the production advisory query are clean. The 239-entry archive is 1,274,811 bytes, has SHA-1 `8987902820a58a3ca3841256456a87078425c9bc`, integrity `sha512-wVvM7CU3DrDhEAoWt3o/ZbBzToGsJ6FT3akqkPD7I+VeR8T05Ck4WiNobaSXXVJ+5/6LM/i0fMJgjCfN81RITw==`, and passes strict inspection, isolated installation, public import, CLI, and all 79 bundled-plugin checks.

## 2026-08-04 — Separate Go command construction, argument roles, and process execution

**Decision.** Add a separate Go CWE-78 model for the exact `os/exec` and `golang.org/x/sys/execabs` packages. Preserve request source, import identity, executable selection, command/interpreter grammar, script and indirect-command positions, immutable selection, command object identity, execution, wrapper propagation, controls, and concrete impact as distinct evidence. Do not report a risky `Command` or `CommandContext` until the same non-reassigned command reaches `Run`, `Start`, `Output`, or `CombinedOutput`.

**Why.** Go's standard library constructs `Path` and `Args` without invoking a shell automatically. That makes a fixed executable with an untrusted ordinary argument materially different from `sh -c`, `cmd /c`, PowerShell `-Command`, or an interpreter code flag. The executable name itself can still select arbitrary code; shell and language interpreters can consume command grammar or attacker-selected scripts; SSH consumes a remote command; and Git/rsync arguments can alter execution through options. CodeQL's high-precision Go query models the command name plus indirect shell-like commands, distinguishes safe Git subcommands, and recognizes a preceding `--` for Git and rsync. The inspected gosec G204 implementation flags unresolved arguments to `Command`, `CommandContext`, execabs, and syscall process APIs at construction time and explicitly notes the shell distinction in a TODO. Exact host modeling can add execution closure and reduce direct-argument false positives while retaining high-value executable, interpreter, remote, and option paths.

**Type and argument boundary.** Require one exact default or aliased import and reject dot, blank, duplicate, fork, and suffix-lookalike identities. `Command` uses argument zero as the executable; `CommandContext` adds context before it. A tainted executable is reported as executable selection. Fixed POSIX shells consume only the string after `-c`, including combined fixed flags; later positional values remain data. `cmd` consumes arguments following `/c` or `/k`, PowerShell consumes values after `-Command`, `-c`, or `-EncodedCommand`, and fixed `.bat` or `.cmd` programs retain their specially parsed arguments. Python, Node, Ruby, Perl, and PHP code flags consume one code string, and an untrusted first non-option without a code flag is retained as script selection. Fixed-host SSH treats following arguments as a remote command. Git's remote-capable subcommands and rsync retain untrusted arguments until an exact preceding `--`; ordinary Git subcommands and option-terminated values are rejected. Exact fixed local or package constants are resolved without leaking another function's local constants into scope.

**Object and execution boundary.** Store risk on the exact `Cmd` returned by `Command` or `CommandContext`, propagate a direct local alias, and clear it on replacement. Same-line and multiline fluent execution uses balanced call boundaries and requires the actual `.` chain. Construction, `LookPath`, `String`, pipe setup, and `Wait` without a proven `Start` are inert. `Start` is itself execution even though a later `Wait` is required for resource cleanup. One unique same-directory/package function with a string parameter can summarize the source-to-construction-to-execution path; duplicate or cross-package candidates are rejected rather than guessed.

**Control and review boundary.** Selecting a complete command from an immutable server-owned map is a deterministic grammar barrier. A preceding `--` is a barrier only for the modeled Git/rsync option semantics, not a universal process sanitizer. Regex and prefix checks, context deadlines, and `LookPath` are candidate controls until validation proves they dominate the exact executable, script, code string, remote command, or option position. Review must establish platform quoting, the special `cmd.exe` and batch-file rules documented by Go, PATH and working-directory resolution, environment and credential inheritance, reachable binaries or scripts, option semantics, sandbox/container boundaries, and the concrete process, secret, filesystem, network, or privilege effect.

**Executable and regression evidence.** The positive fixture passes an indexed HTTP value through one unique same-package wrapper, formats it into `sh -c` grammar, and executes it through `CombinedOutput`; its test copies the running Go test executable to a temporary `sh` path and activates a bounded witness that exposes an internal signing key only when injected grammar is present. The matched control retains the handler, wrapper, exact package, shell, flag, finisher, witness, and attack bytes but uses the value only as a key into an immutable map of complete commands. Focused regressions cover the perfect manifest and cross-file path, all four execution methods, inert construction and pipe setup, assigned aliases and clearing, context positions, direct argument controls, POSIX and Windows shells, immutable constants, PowerShell and language interpreters, script paths, Git/rsync `--`, fixed-host SSH, immutable maps, aliases and execabs, import ambiguity and lookalikes, unique-wrapper proof, controls, comments and strings, and reviewer guidance.

**Acceptance evidence.** The focused lane passes 17 tests and 68 assertions, and the eight-model Go SSRF/process/database adjacency gate passes 132 tests and 711 assertions. Both cross-platform Go modules execute successfully. The authoritative Windows suite passes 925 tests with 19 intentional skips, no failures, and 7,358 assertions across 114 files. Formatting, generated models, TypeScript checking, the clean build, Go formatting and execution, and the production advisory query are clean. **Consequence.** The scanner gains a typed Go process lane that is broader than shell-string-only matching and more precise than construction-time unresolved-argument matching. ## 2026-08-04 — Preserve GORM generics signatures and callback execution closure

**Decision.** Extend the existing exact `gorm.io/gorm` CWE-89 model through the generics API without treating it as a spelling variant of the traditional `*gorm.DB` API. Prove `gorm.G[T](db)` from an exact typed database handle, track the documented generic interfaces and aliases, preserve context, grammar, values, expressions, builder state, callbacks, and finishers as separate evidence, and keep the traditional signatures unchanged.

**Why.** GORM has promoted its generics API since v1.30. Its type families materially change the call contract: `Exec` places context before SQL; `Raw` returns an execution interface; query finishers take context and return typed results; `Count` accepts a structural column after context; joins and preloads accept typed builder callbacks; and `Set` returns specialized create/update interfaces. The current upstream source uses DryRun statement construction for `Build`, so materialization is not database dispatch. The inspected CodeQL extension groups modern and legacy GORM receivers around traditional `DB` methods but does not describe `G[T]`, the generic interfaces, their context-first positions, or callback closure. Reusing traditional argument offsets would miss immediate generic SQL, invent inline-condition findings on typed result methods, and confuse join metadata with raw SQL. An exact host model can close those gaps while retaining stronger construction-to-execution precision.

**Parser and type boundary.** Extend the shared Go call parser to consume one balanced generic type-argument list between a callable name and its opening parenthesis, leaving the parsed callable name unchanged. Typed receiver discovery opts into generic type arguments explicitly; non-generic models retain their previous declarations. The GORM model recognizes only the exact upstream import and `gorm.G[T]`, requires its first argument to be a proven `*gorm.DB` or a direct `Debug`, `Session`, `Unscoped`, or `WithContext` derivation, and tracks exact `Interface`, `CreateInterface`, `ChainInterface`, `ExecInterface`, `SetCreateOrUpdateInterface`, or `SetUpdateOnlyInterface` identities. Ordinary `any`, fake receivers, forks, lookalikes, invalid imports, and reassigned generic values are rejected.

**Grammar, value, and execution boundary.** Generic `Exec(ctx, sql, values...)` executes grammar at argument one. `Raw(sql, values...)` retains grammar only until `Scan`, `First`, `Last`, `Take`, `Find`, `FindInBatches`, `Row`, or `Rows`; chain grammar in `Where`, `Not`, `Or`, `Select`, `Distinct`, `Group`, `Having`, `Order`, and `Table` similarly requires a real generic finisher. `Count(ctx, column)` treats the column as grammar. The generic API does not inherit traditional inline-condition or `Pluck` assumptions. Typed `JoinTarget` and preload association arguments are metadata, but exact `JoinBuilder` callbacks can add `Where`, `Not`, `Or`, or `Select`, and exact `PreloadBuilder` callbacks can additionally add `Order`. Placeholder arguments, maps, structs, and later expression arguments remain values. A `gorm.Expr` in constructor options, immediate execution values, or `Set` assignments retains only its first grammar argument. `Build` remains inert because it materializes through DryRun rather than dispatching.

**Executable and regression evidence.** The benchmark adds a generic positive and matched control beside the traditional pair. Both use a minimal local GORM module whose constructor has the official `...clause.Expression` option boundary and whose typed `Find(ctx)` dispatches through a deterministic `database/sql` driver. The positive carries an indexed HTTP query value through one unique wrapper, formats it into a `Where` predicate, and exposes an internal signing key. The control retains the same handler, wrapper, generic constructor, adapter, driver, and attack bytes but keeps the predicate fixed and binds the request value. Focused regressions cover the four-case perfect manifest, exact cross-file path, generic parsing, typed constructor proof, immediate execution, every deferred finisher and clause, `Count`, placeholders and containers, aliases and reassignment, typed parameters and fields, expression-bearing options and `Set`, join/preload callbacks and safe callback values, inert materialization, invalid pseudo-signatures, lookalikes, reviewer guidance, and preservation of all traditional behavior.

## 2026-08-04 — Require Squirrel query grammar to reach a proven runner and execution

**Decision.** Add a separate Go CWE-89 model for the exact modern `github.com/Masterminds/squirrel` package. Preserve request source, import and type identity, immutable builder state, structural grammar, bound values, nested `Sqlizer` values, runner attachment, materialization, preparation, execution, wrapper propagation, and candidate controls as distinct evidence. Do not report query construction, `RunWith`, `ToSql`, `MustSql`, or preparation without the later operation that can execute the exact state.

**Why.** Squirrel's source separates builder construction from dispatch. Builders return new values, `RunWith` stores a `BaseRunner`, and builder `Exec`, `Query`, `QueryRow`, or `Scan` methods materialize and dispatch through that runner. Package helpers accept a runner and `Sqlizer`; `ToSql` and `MustSql` only return text plus arguments. `Where` and `Having` accept either SQL strings or map/Eq-like value containers; `Values`, `Set`, and later placeholder arguments remain data unless an exact nested `Sqlizer` introduces grammar. Upstream explicitly warns that executing `DebugSqlizer` output containing untrusted input is insecure because the debug function interpolates arguments. The current visible CodeQL extension models many Squirrel construction positions, including older package identities, but attaches SQL-injection sinks to construction arguments and leaves variadic-position TODOs; it does not encode the complete builder-to-runner, helper, materialized-query, prepared-statement, or debug-output execution closure. The current gosec SQL taint table names only standard-library DB and Tx methods. Exact host-side closure can therefore improve recall and precision without treating every builder string or same-named method as executed SQL.

**Type, grammar, and immutable-state boundary.** Require one exact import under the default or an explicit alias and reject dot, blank, duplicate, legacy, fork, and suffix-lookalike identities. Proven builders include exact Select, Insert, Replace, Update, Delete, StatementBuilder, and Case constructors; typed builder parameters or fields; and non-reassigned local aliases. Proven runners include exact Squirrel runner interfaces and caches, `WrapStdSql`/`WrapStdSqlCtx`, cache proxies with a proven underlying handle, plus typed or locally inferred `database/sql` DB, Tx, and Conn values. Constructors and structural methods preserve every string grammar position, including all variadic Columns, Options, GroupBy, and OrderBy values; joins, tables, aliases, prefixes, suffixes, and update columns; the first `Expr` argument; each `ConcatExpr` string; the alias argument of `Alias`; and Case/When/Else expressions. **Execution and materialization boundary.** `RunWith` proves only runner attachment. A direct or assigned builder emits only at `Exec`, `Query`, `QueryRow`, `Scan`, or a Context variant with a proven attached runner. Exact `ExecWith`, `QueryWith`, and `QueryRowWith` helpers and their Context forms require both the documented runner position and the exact builder argument. `ToSql` and `MustSql` retain grammar only under the exact returned string identity and emit when that text reaches a typed standard-library database operation or a prepared statement that later executes. `DebugSqlizer` can additionally promote bound values through interpolation, but only if its exact output reaches execution. The assignment that receives an executed fluent chain is explicitly cleared as a builder, preventing a later unrelated `sql.Rows.Scan` from inheriting Squirrel state.

A fixed server-owned selection is a local grammar barrier. ## 2026-08-04 — Require GORM fragments to reach a real finisher

**Decision.** Add a separate Go CWE-89 model for the exact modern `gorm.io/gorm` module. Preserve source, import and receiver identity, query-fragment positions, fluent or assigned builder identity, execution closure, wrapper propagation, and candidate controls as separate evidence. Report immediate `Exec` grammar directly, but retain `Raw` and chain-clause grammar only when the same non-reassigned `*gorm.DB` reaches a documented query or mutation finisher.

**Why.** GORM's official security guidance distinguishes placeholder-bound values from methods whose string arguments become SQL grammar, including `Raw`, `Exec`, `Where`, `Order`, `Table`, joins, inline conditions, `Pluck`, and `gorm.Expr`. Its implementation clones DB state and adds clauses during chain calls; query, row, delete, create, update, and raw callbacks run at finishers. Treating every construction call as executed overstates inert builders and loses the object-identity evidence needed to explain the path. The visible CodeQL data extension lists the main GORM fragment arguments but attaches sinks directly to those calls. Explicit finisher closure can therefore improve precision while adding official positions absent from that list, notably `InnerJoins`, inline conditions, and expression grammar.

**Type, grammar, and execution boundary.** Require one exact upstream import under the default or an explicit alias and reject blank, dot, duplicate, legacy, fork, and suffix-lookalike bindings. Proven receivers include typed parameters, package or current-function declarations, unique same-file method-receiver fields, exact `gorm.Open` results, and locally derived sessions or transactions. Preserve same-line and multiline direct chains as well as assigned builders through nonexecuting derivation. `Raw`, `Where`, `Not`, `Or`, `Select`, `Distinct`, `Table`, `Group`, `Having`, `Order`, `Joins`, and `InnerJoins` consume grammar at argument zero; additional `Select` or `Distinct` arguments are structural columns unless the first string supplies their expression placeholders. `Exec` consumes immediate SQL at argument zero. `Find`, `First`, `Last`, `Take`, `Delete`, `FirstOrCreate`, and `FirstOrInit` treat argument zero as destination/model and an optional argument one as condition grammar; later arguments and request data confined to map or struct conditions remain bound values. `Pluck` consumes identifier grammar at argument zero. The first `gorm.Expr` argument is expression grammar when used by `Exec`, `Create`, `CreateInBatches`, `Save`, `Update`, `UpdateColumn`, `Updates`, or `UpdateColumns`, while later expression arguments remain values. Reassignment clears taint, receiver, builder, expression, and bound-container identities; fixed server-owned fragment maps remain safe selections.

**Control and review boundary.** Manual quote replacement, regex fragment checks, deadlines, GORM prepared-statement mode, the global-update guard, DryRun, and the presence of later bound arguments are retained as candidate controls rather than universal sanitizers. DryRun is strong counterevidence only if validation proves it dominates the complete builder-to-finisher path and no non-dry consumer executes the statement. Correction must establish dialect placeholder behavior, exact identifier or table allowlisting, statement stacking, role and transaction privileges, tenant predicates, returned columns, write reachability, and the concrete unauthorized data or state effect.

**Executable and regression evidence.** The positive fixture passes an indexed HTTP query value through one unique same-package wrapper, formats it into SQL, and executes it through `Raw(...).Scan(...)`; an offline signature-compatible GORM subset over a deterministic `database/sql/driver` witness proves the injected predicate exposes an otherwise internal signing-key record. The matched control retains the handler, wrapper, receiver, adapter, driver, and attack bytes but keeps the SQL template fixed and supplies the input only after a placeholder. **Consequence.** The scanner covers common traditional GORM v2 raw SQL, structural clause, inline condition, identifier, and expression paths without conflating placeholder values with grammar or construction with execution.
