## 2026-08-04 — Model sqlx query grammar without confusing named values or preparation with execution

**Decision.** Add a separate Go CWE-89 model for the exact `github.com/jmoiron/sqlx` module. Preserve typed DB, Tx, and Conn handles; receiver and package helper signatures; destination, context, query, and value positions; placeholder/named transformations; preparation; statement transfer; execution; wrapper propagation; and candidate controls as separate evidence. A tainted prepare is retained only after the same non-reassigned extended statement executes.

**Why.** Sqlx embeds `database/sql` but materially changes important callable shapes. `Select` and `Get` place a destination before query text; context variants add context first. Package helpers accept Queryer or Execer interfaces, while receiver methods operate on concrete sqlx handles. `NamedExec` and `NamedQuery` bind a map or struct after the query, `Named` and `BindNamed` return transformed SQL plus arguments, and `Rebind` changes placeholder dialect without authorizing query grammar. `Preparex` and `PrepareNamed` return extended statements, and transactions can wrap statements through `Stmtx` or `NamedStmt`. Treating every argument or every preparation as a sink would therefore create both false positives and false execution claims. Official CodeQL guidance supports exact callable and argument-access modeling, while the inspected gosec SQL taint sink table explicitly lists standard-library DB and Tx operations without sqlx signatures.

**Type, argument, and execution boundary.** Require one exact upstream import under a default or explicit alias and reject dot, blank, duplicate, fork, and suffix-lookalike bindings. Proven receivers include typed parameters, package or current-function declarations, unique same-file receiver fields, exact Open/Connect/NewDb constructors, Beginx/BeginTxx/MustBegin derivatives, Connx, and Unsafe. Receiver and package helper signatures each preserve their documented query position. Package helpers additionally require an exact local proven sqlx executor argument rather than accepting an arbitrary interface implementation. Later positional values, the final NamedExec/NamedQuery map or struct, and statement execution arguments remain data. `Rebind`, `Named`, and `BindNamed` propagate taint only from their query input. Prepare/Preparex/PrepareNamed and context/package forms store a tainted query under the exact returned statement identity; execution or a typed transaction transfer followed by execution closes the path. Reassignment clears queries, receivers, and statements.

**Executable and regression evidence.** The positive fixture passes an HTTP query value through one unique same-package wrapper, formats it into SQL, and sends it to destination-before-query `DB.Select`; a deterministic local sqlx-compatible adapter over `database/sql` proves an injected predicate returns an otherwise internal signing-key record. The matched control retains the handler, wrapper, receiver, destination, driver, and attack bytes but keeps SQL fixed and passes the value after a placeholder. Fourteen focused tests cover receiver and package signatures, positional and named values, exact aliases and types, constructors and derived handles, fields, preparation and statement transfer, rebinding and named transformation, reassignment and fixed selection, wrapper ambiguity, comments and strings, controls, and reviewer guidance.

**Acceptance evidence.** The focused lane passes 14 tests and 98 assertions, and the five-model Go SSRF/database adjacency gate passes 69 tests and 388 assertions. Both offline fixture modules execute successfully. The authoritative Windows suite passes 862 tests with 19 intentional skips, no failures, and 7,035 assertions across 111 files. Built-output review emits one exact positive at `search.go:12`, preserves the request source at `handler.go:10`, wrapper call at `handler.go:11`, string parameter at `search.go:9`, and query construction at `search.go:10`, and emits nothing for the placeholder control or production SDK source. Formatting, generated models, TypeScript checking, the clean build, Go formatting, and the current production advisory query are clean. The 227-entry archive is 1,205,187 bytes, has SHA-1 `a3caa55d8f361d68ef0cd9771010b3f072dd0cb0`, integrity `sha512-AW6LkrSD455Opi4iN58LSmeVnh77trfccagByjb3xAjvWCT16rN1yovQ8CNPWjQsGaC8N1NroVELMlMlgUfyZg==`, and passes strict inspection, isolated installation, public import, CLI, and all 79 bundled-plugin checks.

**Consequence.** The scanner covers common sqlx direct, helper, named, and deferred query paths without weakening exact-type proof or conflating values with query grammar. ## 2026-08-04 — Execute custom pgx rewrites only through their first returned SQL value

**Decision.** Extend the existing typed pgx v5 CWE-89 model with bounded summaries for exact local custom `QueryRewriter` implementations. Preserve the custom type, pointer/value method set, receiver field, original SQL parameter, assignment, first returned expression, leading option position, query or batch operation, and final dispatch as separate evidence. Treat attacker data confined to the second returned value as bound arguments rather than query grammar.

**Why.** Pgx v5.10 defines `QueryRewriter` as `RewriteQuery(context.Context, *Conn, string, []any) (string, []any, error)`. Its `Exec` and `Query` implementations consume recognized leading options, remove the rewriter, call that method, and execute the returned SQL with the returned arguments. `SendBatch` performs the same transformation for each queued query before protocol dispatch. The prior model mentioned custom rewrites only as reviewer work, which missed SQL assembled behind an apparently fixed or empty query argument. Conversely, treating every field or returned argument as SQL would report the primary safe use of rewriters: fixed placeholder grammar plus a returned value slice. Visible construction-oriented community rules do not prove this return-value or queue-to-dispatch boundary. Modeling the documented interface operation therefore improves recall without collapsing SQL and value roles.

**Type and summary boundary.** Require one exact `github.com/jackc/pgx/v5` and `context` binding in the method file, one unique struct declaration in the same directory and package as the caller, one unique `RewriteQuery` method, the exact four parameter types, and the exact three return types. Resolve explicit aliases and Go's `any`/`interface{}` equivalence. Respect pointer method sets: a pointer receiver requires an addressable or pointer instance, while a value receiver accepts either. Reject v4, forks, suffix lookalikes, dot imports, non-struct and cross-package types, duplicate declarations or methods, and inexact signatures rather than guessing. The shared Go function parser now preserves return signatures and skips anonymous `interface{}` or `struct{}` type bodies while locating the real function body.

**Flow and dispatch boundary.** Seed each exact receiver field separately, propagate it through bounded string assignments, and inspect only the first value of each return statement. Independently summarize preservation of the incoming SQL parameter so a pass-through rewriter cannot hide tainted original SQL; a fixed replacement first value suppresses that original flow. At callers, support named-field composite literals, direct composites, value or pointer variables, simple aliases, explicit declarations, and field assignment. Whole-instance or field reassignment clears the old flow. For direct operations, a recognized custom rewriter may follow only exact leading pgx query options and closes at `Exec`, `Query`, or `QueryRow`. For batches it must be the first queued option, and the rewritten path remains deferred until the exact non-reassigned batch reaches `SendBatch`. Built-in named and struct argument rewriters retain their separate value semantics.

**Benchmark evidence.** The strict pgx manifest now contains two matched exploit/control pairs. The new positive places an HTTP form value in a custom rewriter field and returns formatted SQL; the exact offline pgx-compatible witness proves the injected predicate discloses `internal-signing-key`. The control preserves the handler, interface, custom type, bytes, and witness, but returns fixed `$1` SQL first and the field only in `[]any`; the attack returns no internal record while `public` still returns `public-status`. Focused regressions cover exact fixture provenance, returned SQL versus returned arguments, preserved versus replaced input SQL, all three direct methods, leading pgx options, value and pointer methods, aliases, direct composites, explicit field assignment, field and instance clearing, batch dispatch, method/import/type ambiguity, non-leading placement, prompt guidance, and prior direct/prepared/batch behavior.

**Acceptance evidence.** The expanded pgx lane passes 19 focused tests and 98 assertions. The four-model shared Go-parser, SSRF, database/sql, pgx, and pgconn adjacency gate passes 55 tests and 288 assertions. All ten offline Go modules in the hosted fixture workflow pass. The authoritative Windows suite passes 848 tests with 19 intentional skips, no failures, and 6,937 assertions across 110 files. Built-output review emits one exact positive at `handler.go:12`, with form source and field construction at `handler.go:11`, receiver field at `query_rewriter.go:14`, string construction at line 15, and first returned SQL at line 16; the matched returned-argument fixture and production source emit none. Formatting, generated models, TypeScript checking, the clean production build, Go formatting, and the production advisory query are clean. The 223-entry archive is 1,194,341 bytes, has SHA-1 `0404b346b1d8ed357a66309864d6bca892637a2f`, integrity `sha512-SPvPTpcSK1PONTD4YyH3gMGLjxDhOsyUE1Dl+vnui6Y1j4laWfswtSiF+JsWW0iIRAH7yBf1N/m2sy20gbIZDA==`, and passes strict archive inspection, isolated installation, public import, CLI, and all 79 bundled-plugin file checks.

**Consequence.** The scanner can now expose a pgx SQL-injection path hidden behind a fixed call-site SQL argument and can explain the cross-file field-to-return transformation, while rejecting the matched safe rewriter. Remaining work includes cross-package rewriter libraries, constructor- and interface-returned instances, positional composite literals, embedded fields, multi-hop method helpers, path-sensitive branch and error analysis, anonymous handlers, pgx v4, sqlx/sqlc, builders and ORMs, stored procedures, and deployment database-role evidence. Each extension must preserve the first returned SQL value, returned argument slice, option ordering, queueing, dispatch, protocol, authorization, and concrete impact as distinct proof obligations.

## 2026-08-04 — Require low-level pgconn work to cross its real protocol dispatch boundary

**Decision.** Add a separate Go CWE-89 lane for the exact `github.com/jackc/pgx/v5/pgconn` package and exact high-level pgx receivers that expose an underlying connection through `PgConn()`. Preserve immediate query execution, COPY commands, named and described preparations, low-level batches, pipelines, wrapper propagation, and candidate controls as separate evidence. Report queued work only after the same non-reassigned object crosses the API operation that can transmit or execute it.

**Why.** Pgx v5.10 documents and implements materially different low-level paths. `PgConn.Exec` sends a simple-protocol query string and permits multiple statements. `ExecParams` sends one extended-protocol command with SQL separate from parameter bytes, OIDs, and format codes. `CopyFrom` and `CopyTo` immediately send their SQL command while the reader or writer carries data. `Prepare` sends Parse/Describe/Sync but does not execute the prepared statement. `pgconn.Batch.Exec*` encodes work that `PgConn.ExecBatch` later transmits. Pipeline `Send*` methods enqueue frontend messages; `Flush` transmits them, `Sync` adds a synchronization point and calls `Flush`, and `Close` returns an unsynchronized-request error instead of silently flushing. Gosec's current SQL taint sink table names only `database/sql` DB and Tx methods. The pinned community Semgrep pgx rule recognizes legacy connection constructors and string construction without low-level protocol closure. CodeQL's visible Go SQL customization delegates to its generic SQL query abstraction and exact extension models, but does not expose a pgconn pipeline closure. A distinct typed model therefore adds concrete coverage without weakening the high-level pgx model or treating every `Exec` method as a sink.

**Source, type, and immediate-sink boundary.** Require an exact typed `*http.Request` source and exact `pgconn`, pgx v5, or pgxpool import identity under a default or explicit alias. Reject v4, forks, suffix lookalikes, dot imports, and untyped methods. Low-level receivers include explicit `*pgconn.PgConn` parameters, package/current-function declarations, unique same-file method-receiver fields, exact `pgconn.Connect`, `ConnectConfig`, `ConnectWithOptions`, and `Construct` results, plus an exact typed `pgx.Conn.PgConn()` result. High-level pool acquisition and `Conn()` derivation remain typed before that escape hatch. `Exec` and `ExecParams` consume SQL after context at argument one. `CopyFrom` and `CopyTo` consume executable SQL at argument two after context plus their data stream. Only the SQL or COPY expression is tainted; parameter bytes, OIDs, formats, readers, writers, statement names without matching preparation, and other arguments are excluded.

**Preparation, batch, and pipeline closure.** A tainted `Prepare` path is retained only when a fixed statement name later reaches `ExecPrepared` on the same non-reassigned PgConn or the exact returned `StatementDescription` later reaches `ExecStatement`. The same statement identity may close through `Batch.ExecStatement` or `Pipeline.SendQueryStatement` only when the batch or pipeline subsequently dispatches. A typed `pgconn.Batch` accumulates direct, named-prepared, or described queries and becomes reportable only at `PgConn.ExecBatch`; fixed prepared names resolve against that dispatch receiver. A pipeline created by `StartPipeline` retains its origin connection, direct query queues, pipeline-local preparations, origin-connection preparations, and statement descriptions. `Flush` or `Sync` emits each pending path once and consumes the queue. Reassignment clears the old queue and origin. `Close` clears pending work and permanently closes that local pipeline identity so later dead sends or synchronization do not create false positives.

**Propagation and control boundary.** Track local request assignments, exact fixed server-owned query-map selection, and one unique receiverless same-package string wrapper in the same directory. Preserve source, wrapper argument, string parameter, query construction, preparation or queue operation, and dispatch positions. Reassignment clears taint and deferred identity. Manual quote replacement, regular-expression fragment checks, deadlines, `PgConn.EscapeString`, and separate parameter bytes remain review leads. `EscapeString` omits surrounding quotes and requires its error to be handled; it does not make arbitrary SQL grammar safe. The reviewer must distinguish simple from extended protocol, multiple-statement support, COPY direction/target, pipeline ordering, connection and transaction state, database roles, tenant predicates, returned or written data, and the concrete unauthorized effect.

**Benchmark and acceptance evidence.** The positive fixture carries an indexed HTTP query value through one same-package wrapper, formats it into simple-protocol SQL, and proves that an injected predicate exposes `internal-signing-key`. The matched control keeps SQL fixed, passes the same bytes only through `ExecParams`, rejects the attack, and still returns `public-status` for the allowed value. The focused lane passes 11 tests and 66 assertions; the nine-file adjacent Go and cross-language framework gate passes 63 tests and 380 assertions; both executable modules pass; and the authoritative Windows suite passes 841 tests with 19 intentional skips, no failures, and 6,903 assertions across 110 files. Built-output review emits one exact positive at `search.go:12` with source at `handler.go:10`, wrapper call at `handler.go:11`, parameter at `search.go:10`, construction at `search.go:11`, and dispatch at `search.go:12`; the control and production source emit none. Formatting, generated models, TypeScript checking, production build, Go formatting, and the production advisory query are clean. The 223-entry archive is 1,181,769 bytes, has SHA-1 `f21693e637b9c8fe0a6c80267e62ef8b505e0ccf`, integrity `sha512-6rHBqmPxn9GFdihos0ztdKcAtybkWVK0aMc6XBRDfGg0NkmpU9EytOdvoNwTEBBcmI9Z0qy91iddgkDDR72JiA==`, and passes strict inspection, isolated installation, public import, CLI, and all 79 bundled-plugin file checks.

**Consequence.** The scanner now covers a lower-level PostgreSQL attack surface that construction-only and `database/sql`-only rules miss, while rejecting inert pipeline and batch state plus bound data that coarse method matching would over-report. Remaining work includes statement descriptions created outside the bounded function, cross-package and multiple wrapper hops, arbitrary connection factories and interfaces, direct frontend protocol messages, high-level `QueryRewriter` summaries, pgx v4, sqlx/sqlc, builders and ORMs, stored procedures, and deployment database-role evidence. Each extension must continue to separate query grammar, protocol transmission, execution, bound data, authorization, and concrete impact.

## 2026-08-04 — Close pgx query grammar through prepared names and batch dispatch

**Decision.** Add a separate Go CWE-89 lane for the exact `github.com/jackc/pgx/v5` and `github.com/jackc/pgx/v5/pgxpool` packages. Emit direct findings only when a typed request source reaches the SQL-text argument of a proven high-level pgx receiver. Preserve context, SQL, later values/options, manual preparation, prepared-name reuse, batch queuing, batch dispatch, wrapper propagation, and candidate controls as distinct evidence. Require a manually prepared query to execute by a fixed name on the same receiver, and require queued query text to reach `SendBatch` through the same non-reassigned typed batch.

**Why.** Current pgx documents `Conn.Exec`, `Query`, and `QueryRow` as context, SQL-or-prepared-name, then positional arguments; `pgxpool` exposes the same order. It automatically prepares and caches ordinary queries, but caching does not change attacker-controlled SQL into data. Manual `Prepare` separates SQL text from the fixed name later accepted by query methods. `Batch.Queue` stores SQL or a prepared name and `SendBatch` performs the dispatch. The available Semgrep community rule is marked low-confidence, recognizes legacy connection patterns and concatenation or `fmt.Sprintf`, and does not prove v5 pool type identity, request provenance, bound-value exclusion, prepared-name closure, or batch dispatch. CodeQL's Go library-model guidance confirms that exact package, receiver, method, and argument positions are the appropriate extension boundary. A typed v5 model can therefore improve both recall and precision without turning every `Query` method into a finding.

**Source, receiver, and argument boundary.** Require exact `net/http` request identity plus exact pgx v5 or pgxpool imports under their default or explicit identifiers. Reject v4, forks, suffix lookalikes, dot and blank imports, duplicate exact imports, and untyped methods. Sources retain query, form, post-form, path, and header access. Receivers include explicit `*pgx.Conn`, `pgx.Tx`, `*pgxpool.Pool`, `*pgxpool.Conn`, and `*pgxpool.Tx` parameters, package or current-function declarations, uniquely declared same-file receiver fields, `pgx.Connect*`, `pgxpool.New*`, acquired connections, and derived transactions/connections. `Exec`, `Query`, and `QueryRow` take SQL or a prepared name at argument one after context; later arguments are values or execution options. Exact pgx named and struct argument rewriters do not recursively compile their values as SQL.

**Preparation and batch boundary.** Retain tainted `Prepare(ctx, name, sql)` text only when `name` is a fixed quoted/raw literal or a non-reassigned local fixed-string binding and the same receiver later executes that name. A different receiver, dynamic or replaced name, receiver replacement, or inert preparation breaks the modeled closure. Track typed `pgx.Batch` parameters, fields, declarations, addressable composites, and `new` results. Preserve each `Queue(query, args...)` query position and later values, but emit only when that exact non-reassigned batch reaches argument one of `SendBatch` on a proven receiver. Resolve a fixed queued prepared name against preparation on the dispatch receiver. Queued-but-unsent text and a batch replaced before dispatch are rejected.

**Propagation, control, and impact boundary.** Follow bounded assignments, fixed server-owned query-map selection, and one uniquely resolved receiver-less same-package string wrapper in the same directory. Clear query, receiver, batch, fixed-name, and prepared state on relevant reassignment. Manual escaping, regex checks, deadlines, pgx read-only transaction options, `Identifier.Sanitize`, separate query arguments, named/struct rewriters, and simple-protocol configuration remain review evidence rather than global sanitizers. `Identifier.Sanitize` can strongly constrain an identifier component but does not authorize arbitrary SQL grammar. Pgx documents that simple protocol performs quoted and escaped client-side interpolation; it still does not make attacker-controlled SQL text safe and may permit multiple statements. Validation must establish protocol mode, custom `QueryRewriter` behavior, stacking, role and transaction privileges, tenant predicates, returned columns, and concrete unauthorized state or data impact.

**Executable and regression evidence.** The positive fixture passes an indexed request value through one same-package function, formats it into `pgxpool.Pool.Query` SQL text, and exposes a deterministic internal signing-key record. The control preserves the handler, wrapper, receiver, and attack bytes but keeps SQL fixed and passes the value after `$1`; the attack yields no internal record while `public` still yields its intended row. Each fixture uses a local module replacement under the exact pgx v5 module path implementing only the documented query signature and deterministic witness semantics, avoiding a database service or dependency download while keeping the scanner's import and type proof exact. Twelve focused tests cover direct and inferred Conn/Tx/Pool receivers, pool aliases, query positions, named values, fixed preparation names and same-receiver closure, prepared batches, queue-to-send closure, package handles, receiver fields, one wrapper, ambiguity, boundaries, v4 and lookalike imports, dot and duplicate imports, untyped methods, reassignment, fixed maps, comments/strings, candidate controls, and reviewer guidance.

**Acceptance evidence.** The focused pgx lane passes 12 tests and 64 assertions; the eight-file adjacent Go and cross-language framework gate passes 55 tests and 297 assertions. Both offline pgx modules pass their executable witnesses. The complete authoritative Windows suite passes 830 tests and 6,837 assertions across 109 files with 19 intentional environment-specific skips and no failures. Built-output review emits one exact record at `search.go:12`, with the indexed request source at `handler.go:11`, wrapper call at `handler.go:12`, string parameter at `search.go:10`, query construction at `search.go:11`, and typed pool dispatch at `search.go:12`; the matched `$1` fixture and production source emit none. Formatting, generated-model checks, TypeScript checking, the clean production build, Go formatting, and the production advisory query are clean. The 219-entry archive has SHA-1 `d85d2fa10e8ed802785a065848fcae411caf8c33` and passes strict inspection, two isolated installations, public import, CLI, and all 79 bundled-plugin file checks. The authenticated full-history remote self-scan remains outside this evidence because repository and reachable-history export requires separate explicit disclosure approval.

**Consequence.** The scanner now covers direct, prepared, and batched pgx v5 SQL grammar with stronger provenance and execution closure than construction-only matching. Remaining work includes anonymous handler functions, multi-hop and cross-package summaries, `AcquireFunc` callbacks, arbitrary interfaces and constructor factories, package-wide immutable state, escaped-string equivalence for prepared names, custom `QueryRewriter` summaries, low-level `pgconn` and pipeline APIs, `CopyFrom` identifier flows, pgx v4 where still deployed, `sqlx`, sqlc wrappers, Squirrel and other builders, GORM/go-pg raw surfaces, and real deployment role evidence. Those extensions must keep SQL grammar, bound values, preparation, queueing, dispatch, protocol behavior, and impact separate.

## 2026-08-04 — Separate Go database query grammar from bound values

**Decision.** Emit a Go CWE-89 hypothesis only when a request value reaches the exact query-text parameter of an exact standard-library `database/sql` operation through a proven `*sql.DB`, `*sql.Tx`, or `*sql.Conn`. Preserve the import, request source, local construction, receiver type, optional wrapper, query argument, preparation, execution, and candidate controls in one typed record. Treat later variadic arguments as values rather than grammar, and require a tainted prepared statement to execute before emission.

**Why.** CodeQL's current high-precision, default-suite `go/sql-injection` query assigns security severity 8.8 and recommends connector parameters instead of concatenation. Current gosec independently covers format-string and concatenated construction through `G201`/`G202` and taint flow through `G701`. The Go standard library makes the critical distinction structural: `DB`, `Tx`, and `Conn` operations receive query text in one declared position and document later arguments as placeholder values. Generic `.Query` matching would report application lookalikes and safe parameter binding; treating `Prepare` as immediate execution would report inert statements. The deterministic host therefore requires exact package and type identity, argument position, and preparation-to-execution closure.

**Source, receiver, and sink boundary.** Require exact `"net/http"` and `"database/sql"` imports under their default or explicit identifiers, and reject dot, blank, or lookalike imports. Sources require a typed `*http.Request` and exact query, form, post-form, path, or header access, including `Values.Get` and indexed `url.Values` forms. Receivers may be typed function parameters, package-level or current-function typed handles, direct `sql.Open`/`OpenDB` results, transactions or connections derived from a proven handle, or an exact `database/sql` field on a uniquely declared same-file method receiver. Another function's same-named local declaration cannot confer type identity. Direct `Exec`, `Query`, and `QueryRow` take query text at position zero; context variants take it at position one. `Stmt` execution arguments are values and never become query-text sinks.

**Preparation and propagation boundary.** `Prepare` and `PrepareContext` retain tainted query grammar but emit only when their exact non-reassigned result later reaches `Stmt.Exec`, `Query`, or `QueryRow`, including context variants. Follow bounded local string assignments and at most one uniquely resolved receiver-less same-package `string` wrapper in the same directory. Bind every call and parameter position and reject duplicate function identities, cross-package or cross-directory guesses, fixed or reassigned query text, prepared-statement replacement, comments, strings, and raw-string examples. An unmodified literal `map[string]string` selection is a fixed-query barrier in the same file. Package-wide map mutation, method-wrapper calls, cross-package summaries, interfaces, query-builder libraries, ORM raw-query APIs, and arbitrary driver helpers remain outside this bounded model.

**Control and impact boundary.** A fixed server-owned query with the request value supplied only through a later argument or `sql.Named` is strong SQL-injection counterevidence, but does not establish authorization or business correctness. Placeholder syntax is driver-specific, and identifiers, sort directions, operators, clauses, and structural fragments normally require exact server-owned selection. Manual quote replacement, regex checks, a deadline, a read-only transaction, and separate arguments attached to an already tainted query remain reviewer leads rather than sanitizers. Validation must establish driver behavior, stacked-statement support, database role privileges, tenant/owner predicates, returned columns, transaction boundaries, and the concrete unauthorized read or write before assigning impact.

**Executable and regression evidence.** The positive module passes a query-controlled status through one same-package function into `fmt.Sprintf` query construction and `DB.QueryContext`. Its standard-library driver witness interprets the resulting predicate and proves the injected `OR 1=1` exposes an internal signing-key row. The matched control preserves the topology and request bytes but keeps query text constant and passes the status only as a placeholder argument; its driver requires the exact fixed query, returns nothing for the attack text, and still returns the intended public row. Twelve focused tests cover the manifest, exact provenance, all direct query positions, parameter and `sql.Named` exclusion, typed and inferred DB/Tx/Conn handles, same-file receiver fields, package globals, aliases, preparation closure and replacement, expanded request sources, fixed selection, reassignment, one wrapper, ambiguity, scope confinement, import and receiver lookalikes, text masking, controls, and reviewer guidance.

**Acceptance evidence.** The focused Go SQL lane passes 12 tests and 60 assertions; the seven-file adjacent Go and cross-language SQL/framework gate passes 43 tests and 233 assertions. Both Go modules pass their executable database witnesses. The complete authoritative Windows suite passes 818 tests and 6,773 assertions across 108 files with 19 intentional environment-specific skips and no failures. Built-output review emits one exact record at `search.go:11`, with the typed request source at `handler.go:10`, wrapper call at `handler.go:11`, string parameter at `search.go:9`, query construction at `search.go:10`, and typed dispatch at `search.go:11`; the matched parameterized fixture and production source emit none. Formatting, generated-model checks, TypeScript checking, a clean production build, Go formatting, and the production advisory query are clean. The 215-entry archive passes strict inspection, two isolated installations, public import, CLI, and all 79 bundled-plugin file checks. The authenticated full-history remote self-scan remains outside this evidence because repository and reachable-history export requires separate explicit disclosure approval.

**Consequence.** The scanner now distinguishes Go SQL grammar from parameter values and inert preparation, improving both recall and precision beyond format-string-only matching. Remaining work includes cross-file method-receiver and package type resolution, interfaces and constructor summaries, multiple wrapper hops, package-wide immutable maps, driver-specific placeholder/stacking metadata, PostgreSQL/MySQL/SQLite driver APIs, `sqlx`, pgx, Squirrel and other query builders, GORM and ORM raw-query surfaces, stored procedures, identifier allowlists, and database-role/deployment evidence. Each extension must retain exact query grammar, bound data, dispatch, and impact as separate proof obligations.

## 2026-08-04 — Require Go request construction to close through dispatch

**Decision.** Emit a Go CWE-918 hypothesis only when a request value from a typed `*http.Request` parameter reaches the complete URL argument of an exact standard-library `net/http` package or client method, or reaches `NewRequest`/`NewRequestWithContext` and that resulting request later reaches a proven `Client.Do`. Preserve the standard-library import identity, source accessor, local assignments, wrapper boundary, request construction, client identity, dispatch, and candidate controls in one typed record.

**Why.** CodeQL's current high-precision `go/request-forgery` query assigns security severity 9.1 and models request data reaching outbound HTTP. Go's standard library separately documents request construction and dispatch: `NewRequest` and `NewRequestWithContext` create a request, while package `Get`/`Head`/`Post`/`PostForm` and `Client` methods perform it. Treating every constructor, `.Get`, or `.Do` token as a sink would confuse inert objects, application lookalikes, and unrelated receivers with network effects. Conversely, modeling only package convenience functions would miss the idiomatic explicit-request path and configured clients.

**Import, source, and sink boundary.** Require an exact `"net/http"` import under its default or explicit identifier and reject dot imports, blank imports, and lookalike paths. Recognize only `URL.Query().Get`, `FormValue`, `PostFormValue`, `PathValue`, and `Header.Get` from a parameter whose declared type resolves textually to `*http.Request` under that import. Preserve only the complete URL argument of `Get`, `Head`, `Post`, and `PostForm`; body and form-data arguments never become destination flow. Accept those methods on the package, `http.DefaultClient`, a typed `http.Client` parameter, or a directly constructed non-reassigned client. For `Do`, require a request created from the tracked URL by `NewRequest` or `NewRequestWithContext`; construction without dispatch is counterevidence.

**Propagation boundary.** Follow bounded local string assignments and aliases, then at most one uniquely resolved same-package wrapper with a `string` parameter. The wrapper may be in the same file or another `.go` file in the same directory and declared package. Bind the exact call argument and parameter position, and reject duplicate function identities, cross-directory or cross-package guesses, reassignment, request replacement, and text inside comments, interpreted strings, raw strings, or rune literals. A literal package-level `map[string]string` selection is a destination barrier only when the map has not been reassigned or mutated in the same file. Package-wide mutation proof, cross-package resolution, method receivers, client factories, custom `RoundTripper` implementations, request cloning, and partial-URL assembly remain outside this bounded model.

**Control boundary.** Preserve fail-closed `CheckRedirect`, `http.ErrUseLastResponse`, exact host allowlisting, network-address validation, custom `DialContext`, and allowed-scheme checks as reviewer leads. Go calls `CheckRedirect` before following a redirect, so redirect rejection does not authorize the first attacker-selected URL and cannot by itself close DNS rebinding, proxy, connection reuse, final socket address, Host header, or TLS identity questions. Only complete server-owned destination selection breaks the deterministic URL flow; every other control requires same-value, ordering, and failure-mode validation.

**Executable and regression evidence.** The vulnerable module passes an HTTP query value across one package-local function boundary, constructs a request with `NewRequestWithContext`, and dispatches it through a constructed `http.Client`; its `httptest` witness observes the mock metadata response. The control accepts the same request shape only as an exact key into a server-owned destination map, rejects arbitrary URLs before request construction, returns `http.ErrUseLastResponse` from `CheckRedirect`, and proves a permitted server redirect cannot reach the mock metadata service. Thirteen focused tests cover import identity, all source and dispatch families, body exclusion, construction-versus-dispatch closure, request replacement, fixed and mutated maps, local and cross-file wrappers, ambiguity, package and directory confinement, lookalikes, lexical masking, candidate controls, the manifest, and reviewer instructions.

**Acceptance evidence.** The focused Go lane passes 13 tests and 66 assertions; the 11-file adjacent framework and SSRF gate passes 61 tests and 326 assertions. Both Go modules pass their real loopback witnesses. The complete authoritative Windows suite passes 806 tests and 6,713 assertions across 107 files with 19 intentional environment-specific skips and no failures. Built-output review emits one exact record at `fetch.go:15`, with the typed request source at `handler.go:9`, the wrapper call and parameter, request construction at `fetch.go:10`, and dispatch at `fetch.go:15`; the matched safe fixture and production source emit none. Formatting, generated-model checks, TypeScript checking, a clean production build, Go formatting, and the production advisory query are clean. The 211-entry archive passes strict inspection, two isolated installations, public import, CLI, and all 79 bundled-plugin file checks. The authenticated full-history remote self-scan remains outside this evidence because repository and reachable-history export requires separate explicit disclosure approval.

**Consequence.** The scanner gains its first typed Go outbound-request lane without broadening generic method names into findings. Remaining work includes package-wide immutable-state proof, multi-hop and cross-package call resolution, method receivers and fields, constructor/factory summaries, custom transports and `RoundTripper`, URL-component propagation, canonical authority parsing, DNS-answer validation, connection-time address pinning, proxy policy, redirect-by-redirect revalidation, and Host/TLS binding. Each extension must preserve request construction versus dispatch and complete destination authority as separate proof obligations.

## 2026-08-04 — Require execution closure on self-hosted pull-request runners

**Decision.** Emit a same-file GitHub Actions CWE-284/CWE-829 hypothesis only when a pull-request-capable event schedules a job on a statically classifiable self-hosted or custom runner, the job checks out attacker-controlled pull-request bytes through the official Checkout action, and a later command or repository-local action executes from the affected workspace. Preserve the exact event, runner selection, checkout, execution sink, effective permissions, structural secret use, and candidate controls in one typed record.

**Why.** GitHub's secure-use guidance distinguishes ephemeral clean GitHub-hosted virtual machines from self-hosted runners, warns that untrusted workflow code can persistently compromise a self-hosted runner, and advises against using self-hosted runners for public repositories. CodeQL's experimental `actions/pr-on-self-hosted-runner` query assigns severity 9.0, high precision, and CWE-284, and CodeQL 2.26.0 updated its standard hosted-runner label classification to reduce false positives. Scheduling alone, however, does not prove that pull-request bytes execute. This implementation adds the missing Checkout-to-workspace-to-execution closure and preserves CWE-829 for the untrusted code-loading boundary.

**Runner and trigger boundary.** Recognize the literal `self-hosted` label even when another label is dynamic, static runner groups, and static custom labels. Exclude CodeQL 2.26.0's current GitHub-hosted Ubuntu, macOS, and Windows label forms and its BuildJet/Warp hosted forms; reject fully dynamic selection because the repository does not prove ownership. Recognize `pull_request`, `pull_request_target`, `issue_comment`, pull-request review and review-comment events, plus an explicitly closed `workflow_run` case. `workflow_run` does not inherit an inferred upstream checkout: it must independently contain an exact recognized pull-request checkout and later execution.

**Checkout, state, and impact boundary.** Under `pull_request`, official Checkout defaults and exact `github.sha`, `github.ref`, or pull-request merge SHA select untrusted merge-ref content. Explicit head SHA and fork repository forms remain recognized for the other supported events, including `github.event.number`, `github.event.issue.number`, and literal pull refs. An explicit fixed repository is trusted unless it is the current repository or an exact fork context. Workspace state follows checkout paths and workflow/job working directories; a clean trusted checkout clears overlapping taint, while `clean: false` does not. Checkout v7's default fork refusal is relevant to explicit privileged fork checkout, not ordinary `pull_request` merge-ref checkout. Ordinary `pull_request` rows never invent secret or write-token impact. Other event rows add those categories only from exact effective permission and structural secret evidence.

**Validation boundary.** Static custom labels and runner groups are candidates, not proof of customer ownership. Review must confirm the job resolves to a customer-controlled runner and examine persistence across later jobs, reusable work directories, service-account authority, signing keys, cloud metadata, internal network services, Docker sockets, tool caches, and release/deployment workloads. Immutable commit selection, read-only current-job permissions, disabled Checkout credential persistence, review labels, and environments constrain current impact but do not erase host compromise. A GitHub-hosted runner or a proven freshly provisioned, single-job JIT runner destroyed after the job is strong persistence counterevidence.

**Executable and regression evidence.** The paired workflows are identical except that the positive uses `[self-hosted, linux, x64]` and the control uses `ubuntu-latest`. The witness models a reusable runner where an untrusted pull-request job overwrites a user-writable release helper and a later privileged job observes a mock token; its hosted control destroys the pull-request machine and starts the later job from a trusted helper on a fresh machine. Thirteen focused tests cover the manifest, exact provenance, explicit/custom/group runners, current standard hosted exclusions, dynamic selection, default and explicit pull refs, event coupling, fixed repository rejection, workspace cleanup, Checkout v7 semantics, privilege categories, controls, strict YAML failure cases, deduplication, and reviewer guidance.

**Acceptance evidence.** The focused lane passes 13 tests and 72 assertions; the six adjacent GitHub Actions lanes pass 64 tests and 356 assertions. All six executable witnesses pass. The complete authoritative Windows suite passes 793 tests and 6,647 assertions across 106 files with 19 intentional environment-specific skips and no failures. Deterministic review emits one exact positive record at `.github/workflows/test.yml:12`, no hosted-control record, and no production-repository record. Formatting, generated-model drift, TypeScript checking, the production build, and the production advisory query are clean. The 207-entry archive passes strict inspection, two isolated installations, public import, CLI, and all 79 bundled-plugin file checks. The external fresh-key self-scan is deliberately not counted: sending the uncommitted repository and 128 reachable commits to Copilot was blocked pending separate explicit disclosure approval.

**Consequence.** The scanner now distinguishes a complete persistent-runner compromise opportunity from mere self-hosted scheduling and from clean hosted execution. Remaining work includes cross-file `workflow_run` provenance, organization runner-group policy resolution, GitHub larger-runner metadata, autoscaler/JIT lifecycle evidence, service-container and Docker-socket reachability, runner-controller configuration, and attack paths from one compromised job into specific later release jobs. Those extensions must preserve exact runner identity, lifecycle, workspace state, and execution closure rather than broadening every custom label into a confirmed vulnerability.

## 2026-08-04 — Bind workflow event fields to generated script source

**Decision.** Emit a same-file GitHub Actions CWE-094/CWE-095/CWE-116 hypothesis only when an exact supported workflow trigger makes an exact event context attacker-controlled and that value reaches a workflow `run` script or a code-interpreting input of one exact known action. Preserve the trigger, source context, optional workflow-expression environment alias, interpreter and input identity, effective permission, structurally available secret, and candidate controls in one typed record.

**Why.** GitHub documents that expressions embedded in inline scripts are substituted before the shell or action interpreter receives the generated program, so source-language quoting does not make attacker text data. Its preferred construction places the expression in an intermediate environment value and reads that value through the interpreter's native environment API. CodeQL rates Actions code injection security severity 9 with very-high precision and CWE-094/CWE-095/CWE-116. The cross-file reusable-workflow and composite-action models did not cover the common direct `${{ github.event.* }}` case. Conversely, a suffix match on event properties or every action `with` value would invent attacker capability and executable sinks. The model therefore couples supported events to exact fields and takes the current action code-input identities from CodeQL and zizmor rather than broadening ubiquitous YAML keys.

**Expression, scope, and sink boundary.** Parse only exact `.github/workflows/*.yml` or `.yaml` paths as strict YAML 1.2 with unique keys and no aliases. The bounded expression evaluator recognizes direct dot or single-quoted bracket contexts, enclosing parentheses, value-preserving `toJSON`, `fromJSON`, `format`, and `join`, and only reachable value-carrying `&&` or `||` arms. It deliberately rejects comparisons, `contains`, prefix/suffix predicates, unknown functions, fixed results, and unreachable short-circuit arms. Compute workflow, job, and step environment shadowing in order: an attacker-derived environment expression is a propagator only if the script later recompiles `${{ env.NAME }}`; a fixed override clears it, and native shell variables or `process.env` remain data. `run` is an interpreter sink. Action inputs are sinks only for one of 18 exact repository identities and its recorded code-bearing input under a literal revision; ordinary action data, unknown inputs, lookalikes, and expression-built revisions are not sinks.

**Privilege and control boundary.** Keep code execution distinct from ambient authority. `pull_request` makes the modeled fields attacker-controlled, but fork runs normally have read-only tokens and no ordinary secrets, so those impact categories are never inferred for that trigger. For default-branch-capable events, write-token impact requires effective write permissions and secret impact requires an inherited exact secret environment value or structural same-step `secrets.*`/`github.token` access. Workflow/job permission overrides, environment shadowing, read-only tokens, label review gates, and deployment environments remain explicit reviewer evidence. None of them silently sanitizes program text, and comments cannot create secret evidence.

**Executable and regression evidence.** The paired workflows receive `pull_request_target`, grant contents/OIDC write capability, and use official GitHub Script with a mock release-token environment. The positive inserts the pull-request title directly into JavaScript source; substitution of the harmless payload creates a second statement that observes the mock token. The control maps the same title into `TITLE` and reads only `process.env.TITLE`, producing one inert logged string. Eleven focused tests cover perfect manifest gates, exact source/sink/impact provenance, ordinary `pull_request` impact reduction, event/field coupling, dot and bracket contexts, bounded functions and short-circuit reachability, boolean-only rejection, all environment scopes and overrides, native use, `run`, all 18 action identities, unknown inputs and lookalikes, effective permissions, secret shadowing, review/environment leads, malformed/duplicate/aliased YAML, non-workflow paths, and reviewer guidance.

**Acceptance evidence.** The focused lane passes 11 tests and 95 assertions; the five adjacent GitHub Actions lanes pass 51 tests and 284 assertions. All five executable witnesses pass. The complete authoritative suite passes 780 tests and 6,575 assertions across 105 files with 19 intentional environment-specific skips and no failures. A managed-sandbox run initially denied the Windows ACL hardening used by temporary scanner homes and caused a synchronized child-process cascade; both representative cases and the complete suite passed at the intended host filesystem boundary without a product change. Deterministic fixture review emits exactly the vulnerable same-workflow expression-compilation path while the native-environment control and production source emit none. A fresh-key 128-commit self-scan examines 1,126 current plaintext files and 2,440 reachable historical plaintext blobs totaling 56,269,962 bytes with zero candidates, complete history, and no truncation. Formatting, generated-model drift, TypeScript checking, the production build, and the production advisory query are clean. The 207-entry archive passes strict inspection, two isolated installations, public import, CLI, and all 79 bundled-plugin file checks.

**Consequence.** Review now receives a trigger-capable event source joined to the actual generated-program boundary instead of a raw expression warning, while GitHub's documented intermediate-environment defense remains a true negative. Additional event payloads, dynamic object indexing, `GITHUB_ENV` propagation, matrices, job outputs, shell-specific escaping, JavaScript-action internals, repository-owned custom actions, remote action versions, and self-hosted runner persistence remain future typed extensions. They should add explicit source capability, value semantics, and interpreter identity rather than weakening the exact event and sink maps.

## 2026-08-04 — Resolve workflow inputs into local composite-action interpreters

**Decision.** Emit a cross-file GitHub Actions CWE-094/CWE-095/CWE-116 hypothesis only when an exact externally influenced default-branch event controls one exact workflow-step input, that step invokes a literal repository-local action directory, exactly one `action.yml` or `action.yaml` in that directory declares the same input and selects `runs.using: composite`, and a runnable composite step interpolates the input or a same-step input-derived workflow-expression environment alias into `run` or official `actions/github-script` source. Preserve trigger, caller field, local action call, descriptor and input identity, optional environment alias, and interpreter sink as one ordered record.

**Why.** GitHub documents that Actions expressions are evaluated and substituted before a generated shell program runs, so quotes around attacker-controlled expression text do not establish a data boundary. Its composite-action tutorial demonstrates the safe construction: assign `${{ inputs.who-to-greet }}` to a step environment entry and read `$INPUT_WHO_TO_GREET` natively. GitHub's metadata reference separately states that composite actions do not receive automatic `INPUT_*` variables, require an `action.yml` or `action.yaml`, declare inputs in metadata, select `using: composite`, and require a shell for `run`. CodeQL rates Actions code injection security severity 9 with very-high precision and CWE-094/CWE-095/CWE-116. A same-file workflow matcher would miss the action descriptor and interpreter boundary; a generic `uses`/`with` matcher would confuse ordinary action data with generated source.

**Identity, flow, and control boundary.** Parse caller workflows and action metadata as strict YAML 1.2 with unique keys and no aliases. Couple each supported event to its exact attacker-controlled fields and accept only a direct caller expression. Normalize literal `./` action directories without resolving absolute, backslash, parent-traversing, or expression-built targets. Reject missing descriptors and the ambiguous case where both metadata names exist. Require action name and description, a declared same-name input with a description, the composite runtime, and a steps sequence. Direct input interpolation and same-step input-derived `${{ env.NAME }}` re-expansion close the code sink; native shell variables and `process.env` do not, and step-local environment taint does not leak into later steps. Require a scalar shell for composite `run`; accept only official semantic-version or full-commit-pinned GitHub Script identity and its `script` field. Preserve effective read-only caller permissions, job and step review labels, and deployment environments as leads. Classify secret exposure only when the caller explicitly forwards a `secrets.*` or `github.token` value under an input name that the exact sink step structurally consumes; YAML comments cannot create that category.

**Executable and regression evidence.** Both paired workflows receive `issue_comment`, grant write/OIDC permissions, and call the same local action with `github.event.comment.body` as `release-name` and a mock release secret as `release-token`. Both metadata files declare the same inputs and official GitHub Script step. The positive inserts `${{ inputs.release-name }}` into JavaScript source; substitution of the harmless payload creates a second statement that observes the mock token. The control maps the same input into that step's `RELEASE_NAME` environment entry and reads `process.env.RELEASE_NAME`; its witness proves one inert logged value and no token-observing statement. **Acceptance evidence.** The focused lane passes 10 tests and 54 assertions; the four adjacent GitHub Actions lanes pass 40 tests and 189 assertions. All four executable witnesses pass. The complete authoritative suite passes 769 tests and 6,480 assertions across 104 files with 19 intentional environment-specific skips and no failures. Deterministic fixture review emits exactly the vulnerable workflow-to-composite-action expression-compilation path while the native-environment control emits none; production source emits no composite-action injection hypothesis. A fresh-key 128-commit self-scan examines 1,121 current plaintext files and 2,424 reachable historical plaintext blobs totaling 55,538,232 bytes with zero candidates, complete history, and no truncation. Formatting, generated-model drift, TypeScript checking, the production build, and the production advisory query are clean. The 207-entry archive passes strict inspection, two isolated installations, public import, CLI, and all 79 bundled-plugin file checks.

**Consequence.** Review now receives a complete workflow-to-local-action code-generation path rather than a caller-only expression alert, while GitHub's documented step-environment defense remains a true negative. Caller/workflow environment aliases, `GITHUB_ENV` propagation, nested composite actions, dynamic shell selection, shell-specific sanitizers, composite outputs recompiled by callers, JavaScript and Docker action internals, remote action repositories, and action metadata replaced by an earlier untrusted checkout remain future typed extensions. They should preserve descriptor identity, step scope, interpreter timing, and exact secret/permission reachability rather than widening ubiquitous action inputs.

## 2026-08-04 — Trace attacker fields through reusable-workflow script compilation

**Decision.** Emit a cross-file GitHub Actions CWE-094/CWE-095/CWE-116 hypothesis only when an exact externally influenced default-branch event controls one exact caller field, a job forwards that value under a literal input name to an exact local `.github/workflows/*.yml` or `.yaml` target, the target declares the same `workflow_call` input as `string`, and a called step interpolates that input or an input-derived workflow-expression environment alias into `run` or official `actions/github-script` source. **Why and rejected alternative.** GitHub documents pull-request titles, issue bodies and comments, branch names, and similar context fields as attacker-controlled and explains that `${{ ... }}` substitution occurs before the resulting inline script is interpreted. Its preferred defense assigns the expression to an intermediate environment variable and consumes that value through the interpreter's native environment API. CodeQL rates the corresponding Actions code-injection query security severity 9 with very-high precision. The preceding research considered cache poisoning, but current GitHub cache policy now blocks default-scope writes from `pull_request_target`, `issue_comment`, and `workflow_run`, while `pull_request` writes remain isolated to the merge ref. A broad cache-save alert would therefore encode superseded platform behavior. Any later cache lane must prove a presently writable scope and a matching executed consumer rather than treating an attempted save as success.

**Identity, flow, and control boundary.** Parse only exact workflow paths as strict YAML 1.2 with unique keys and no aliases. Couple each supported event to its own exact untrusted fields and accept only a direct field expression at the caller, rejecting fixed values and boolean or opaque transformations. Resolve only literal local workflow calls, exact forwarded and declared names, and string input types. In the called workflow, compute workflow/job/step environment shadowing; direct input interpolation and input-derived `${{ env.NAME }}` re-expansion close the code sink, while a fixed step override clears the alias and native `$NAME`, `%NAME%`, PowerShell environment, or `process.env` data access does not. Accept official semantic versions or full commit pins for `actions/github-script`, reject lookalikes and ordinary action arguments, and intersect explicit caller/callee read-only permissions before classifying write-token impact. Classify a secret only when the caller actually inherits or maps it and the called job actually references it. Retain mutable label and deployment-environment gates as review evidence rather than automatic authorization.

**Executable and regression evidence.** The paired callers both receive `issue_comment`, grant write/OIDC permissions, inherit secrets, and forward `github.event.comment.body` as `release-name` to the same local reusable workflow. The positive inserts `${{ inputs.release-name }}` into GitHub Script source; substitution of the harmless payload creates a second JavaScript statement that observes a mock release token. The control assigns that same expression to `RELEASE_NAME` and reads `process.env.RELEASE_NAME`; its witness proves one inert logged value and no token-observing statement. The 207-entry archive passes strict inspection, isolated installation, public import, CLI, and all 79 bundled-plugin file checks.

**Consequence.** Review now receives a complete caller-to-called-workflow code-generation path rather than a same-file string match, and the official intermediate-environment defense is represented without blessing later workflow-expression re-expansion.
